American oil concerns to agree among themselves to
refrain from supplying oil to the Japanese. Mr. Teagle
said that no arrangement of that character could be
made. He stated that the Associated oil group in
California has a 50 per cent interest in an oil refinery
in Japan and is under a long-term contract to supply
crude oil to the refinery; that he understood that the
Associated had been called upon by its Japanese associates
to subscribe its share of the cost of providing the
installations required under the new Japanese law and
that the Associated is planning to supply its share of
the cost, although he (Mr. Teagle) had suggested to the
Associated that it should out of regard for its own
interests postpone making a decision until the situation
becomes more clear. Mr. Teagle believed that the only
practicable method of concluding an arrangement of the
character suggested would be to take action through the
P. & C. Committee (Planning and Co-ordination Committee,
Petroleum Board, Department of the Interior).
I requested that Mr. Teagle regard as strictly con-
fidential the information which I would proceed to give
him. I stated that there is being drafted a tentative
text for representations which might be made to the
Japanese Government in the event that decision is made
to take such action. I stated that it is our understanding
that

concurrence.
In taking his leave, Mr. Teagle repeated that he
would again call on me as soon as he had heard from
Sir Henri Deterding.

Mr. Teagle called me on the telephone from New York.
He said that, since his conversation with me on Septem-
ber 13, Sir Henri Deterding's representative in New York
had talked twice by telephone with representatives of his
company in London. On the basis of what they said, it
appears that the matter under consideration is in a
situation of "stalemate": it seems that the American
Government is waiting on the British Government for aotion
and the British Government waiting on the American Govern-
ment.
I replied that I did not understand how the British
Government could be waiting on us for aotion: they had
FILED
approached us asking us with regard to our attitude; we
had replied that we felt that the situation was a serious
situation, warranting action, and that we would be ready to
give sympathetio consideration to suggestions whioh they
might make for action. Since that exchange of communications,
which
F/G

=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.95)
-2 -
which was on their initiative, they had given us no further
word or indication of their thought or intent. Mr. Teagle
said that they seemed to be waiting on us. I referred to
a previous statement which he had given me wherein his
informant in London had stated that the British Foreign
Orfice had stated that it had "already protested"; I
reminded him that I had suggested at the time that there
must be some confusion with regard to that point; and I
said that it would perhaps be helpful to have a date or
approximate date on whioh the British Government "had
protested". I said that they had not given us any informa-
tion to that effect, nor had they indicated what might be
the nature of their action, if and when.
Mr. Teagle said that he would get in touch with his
London people again on that point.

Mr. Hornbeck was leaving Washington at midnight.)

that it now appeared that the various British authorities
concerned were conferring among themselves with regard to
possible courses of action: they had apparently not yet
FTED

within a few days.

F/G

There followed a disoussion of provisions in the Japa-

and of possible results. Mr. Hornbeck inquired whether it
might not be possible for the oil companies to go consider-
ably further than they had yet gone in their own discussions
of the matter with the Japanese authorities: might they not
intimate to the Japanese that it would be quite impossible
for them to meet the conditions implicit in the law and that,
if the Japanese insisted on their doing so, they would have
to cease doing business in Japan, and, if compelled to take
a loss upon their investments and to give up the prospects
there, they would feel warranted in making it difficult, as
far as lay within their power, for the Japanese to obtain
the supplies of crude oil, et cetera, which it was absolutely
necessary for them to procure abroad. Mr. Teagle did not
commit himself on that point but made some observations with
regard to difficulties and developments in the international
trade in petroleum and made some reference to the idea of
possible advantage to those countries which possess supplies
of oil were there to be imposed everywhere export duties on
orude oil.
Mr. Hornbeok said that we had been giving quite a little
time to the study of the question as now presented in conneo-
tion with Japan and that in course thereof he had discovered
that in France the foreign trade in petroleum had been sub-
jooted to onerous conditions. Mr. Teagle said that such was
the

the case and that we were likely to hear shortly that some-

that in the case of France the oil interests themselves
had handled the matter very badly and he intimated that if
they had it to do over their course of action would be
different.
At that point Mr. Hornbeck inquired whether it might
not be possible for British, Dutch and American oil interests,
if they would effectively cooperate, to control the export
market to such an extent that they might be able to dictate
to governments of non-producing countries which impose

to hor.

MEMO RE PETROLEUM INDUSTRY
CONTROL LAW - JAPANESE EMPIRE
While we have endeavored to keep the State Department
fully advised of current developments in respect to the Petroleum
Industry Control Law, and while we also believe that the
American Embassy at Tokyo has taken similar steps, nevertheless,
we think it now advisable to briefly sumnarize the situation from
the time of promulgation of this law to the present date.
The Petroleum Industry Control Law was enacted March 27,
1934, was promulgated by Imperial Ordinance March 27, 1934, and
became effective on July 1, 1934. On June 27, 1934 Regulations
were adopted for the enforcement of the law. The oil industry is
therefore governed by the Petroleum Industry Control Law, the
Imperial Ordinance and the Regulations,all of which will be referred
to in this memorandum collectively as "the law".
The law applies to importers and refiners of petroleum
products and places practically absolute control of the business
of refining, importing and marketing of petroleum products in
the hends of the Government. Refiners and importers are required
to obtain a yearly license, which the Government may grant, modify
or withhold. They are required to segregate and keep in stock,
in addition to the stocks required to meet the current demands of
their business, such quantities (based upon importations during
speoified past periods) of fuel oil, diesel oil, gasoline and other
petroleum produots as shall be prescribed by the Government,
which stocks they are required to sell to the Government at any
time on the basis of ourrent prices, notwithstanding that the
Government itself is given power to fix selling prices. The

=== Page 17 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
additional storage capacity required for the stocks which must be
thus segregated and carried must be provided by the refiners and
importers at their own expense and risk, and the Government has
the right to designate the place where such stocks must be carried
irrespective of whether the refiner or importer has land or storage
facilities available elsewhere. The Government may determine and
vary from time to time the amount of any produot which a refiner
or importer may import, refine or sell, and is given power to
require refiners and importers to effect extension or improvement
of their equipment. The Government is given the broadest visitor-
ial powers with respect to both business and plans and may require
disclosure of all details of the business. Persons carrying on
business of importing or refining at the time the law goes into
force are deemed to have obtained permission in accordance with the
law, but are required to file with the Government details of their
plan of operations for a specif ied future period, inc luding volume
of products to be imported or marketed during that period, which
plan unless modified with the Government's consent, must be carried
out irrespeotive of the demands of the person's business. The
Government has power to modify any plan filed and require it to be
carried out in its modified form regardless of the wishes of the
refiner or importer.
SPECIFICOBJECTIONS
The principal provisions of the law to which we take
exceptions and the grounds upon whioh we base our objections are

=== Page 18 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
as follows:
1. The provisions requiring us to segregate and carry in stock
a volume of produots several times in exoess of our require-
ments. See Articles 5, 6 and 7 of the Control Law; Articles
7 and 8 of the Imperial Ordinanoe, and Articles 16, 17 and
21 of the Regulations.
We carry normally a three months' supply of products.
Under the law we are required by April 1, l935 to carry a three
months' extra supply, and by October, l935 we are required to
inorease this to six months' supply. e must furnish the addi-
tional storage facilities for this stock at our own expense, and
provide the storage at the place indicated by the Government.
We are required to sell these stooks to the Government at any
time at current prices regardless of whether this price is below
our laid-down cost in Japan, or below the basis of world prices,
and notwithstanding the fact that the Government has the power
to fix the selling prices. This surplus stock must be carried at
all times, which means that if the Government exercises its right
to purohase an existing surplus stock, we must replenish the stock
which the Government may in turn again require us to sell to it
at current prices. No restrictions are placed upon the number
of times the Government may exeroise this right and require us to
replenish the surplus stocks, and no limitations are placed upon
the prices to be fixed by the Government. On the basis of our
present volume of business and present prices, we estimate that
it will cost us approximately 1,900,000 to supply the surplus
stooks which we are required to carry and store for the Govern-
ment's account, and approximately 375,000 (exclusive of the ∞st
of the land) to provide the addit ional storage facilities necessary

=== Page 19 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
therefor.
2. The provisions giving the Government the power to ensure the
supply of a quantity of petroleum, or to take other measures
in order to regulate the demand and supply of petroleum, and
to require refiners or importers to effect an extension or
improvement of their equipment. See Article 7 of the Control
Law.
These provisions are broad enough, when considered in
connection with the power of the Government to modify the plan of
operations submitted by a Company, to require a Company to provide
a supply of any product largely in excess of its needs, and to
provide the necessary storage facilities therefor. This provision
1s in addition to the one which requires the carrying of surplus
stooks above referred to. No limitation is placed upon the
measures which the Government may deem necessary in order to regu-
late the demand and supply of petroleum.
3. The provisions giving the Government the power to order a
refiner or importer to report the details with reference to
his business and operations, and to issue orders or take
other measures deemed necessary for the purpose of super-
vising the importer's or refiner's business, and the provis-
ions giving visitorial power to Government agents. See
Article 10 of the Control Law; Articles 9, 10, 11 and 29 of
the Regulations.
These provisions not only require the disclosure by a
refiner or importer of all of the details of his business, in-
cluding many matters heretofore generally regarded as essentially
private and confidential, but also gives the administrative officers
of the Government the power to issue orders, undefined in soope,
for supervising the business and to examine the condition of the
business, account books, documents "and any other objects". It
is true that the Government, in connection with its Income Tar

=== Page 20 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
Laws, has the right to require disclosure of some of this informa-
tion, but the disclosure of information to taxing authorities is
muoh different than the disclosure to administrative officers in
control of an industry. In the latter case there is real danger
that the infomation thus disclosed may come to the knowledge of
and be used by competitors to our disadvantage.
In connection with the power to be exercised by the
Government in the control of our business, there is no assurance
that we will be permitted either to retain our present volume of
business or to share in any normal increase. That there is real
danger in this situation is evidenced by the comparatively recent
action of the governmental authorities in reducing our quota under
the existing gasoline cartel arrangement. Our quota and that of
the Rising Sun Petroleum Company (British interests) was reduced,
whereas the quotas of the Japanese nationals, who were members of
the cartel, were increased by the governmental authorities. This
was commented upon in our letter to the State Department dated
August 28, 1934.
4. The provisions requiring the filing of plans and detailed
information with respect to future operations of the
business over stated periods in order to obtain a license,
and giving the power to the Government to withhold the
issuanoe of a permit. See Articles 1 and 2 of the Control
Law; Artiole 3 of the Imperial Ordinance, and Articles 1,
2, 5, 6 and 11 of the Regulations.
The requirement of a license would be unobjeotionable
were it not for its limited duration (one year), the restrictions
surrounding its issuance, and the lack of any assurance of
continuity. If a lioense is to be required it should be issued
without the necessity of complying with onerous conditions and

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.98)
-8-
should remain in effeot for a long enough time to give us a
reasonable opportunity to amortize our investment. Our Company
and its predeoessors have been engaged in business in Japan for
noarly forty years. It has, in round numbers, about twelve million
dollars of capital invested in the Empire, and has built up a
substantial business and created a demand for products whioh are
marketed under its trade marked brands. Furthermore, as above
indioated, it will be necessary that we invest approximately
375,000 in order to provide the addi tional storage facilities
required to carry surplus stooks for the benefit of the Government,
and the investment tied up by the necessity of carrying this surplus
stock would approximate an additional 1,900,000. The withholding
of, or refusal to renew, a license would destroy our business and
jeopardize our entire investment. Under Article 3 of the Ordinance,
the Minister of Commerce and Industry is given the power to refuse
a license "when, he taking into consideration the conditions of
the demand for and the supply of oil, considers the grant of the
permit likely to hinder the healthy development of the oil industry".
The power thus given may be arbitrarily exercised and there is no
provision for judicial review. The provisions of Article ll of
the Regulations requiring the disclosure of a plan for the dis-
tribution of profits, and the provisions of Article 29 of the
Regulations giving the Minister of Commeroe and Industry the power
to order a change in the quantity to be imported or the quantity
to be sold, as set forth in the plans filed by the Company, are
partioularly objeotionable. It is difficult to forecast in
advance how profits earned in Japan will be distributed, because

=== Page 22 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
distribution of profits is dependent not alone upon profits made
in Japan but profits made elsewhere, and on the general financial
oondition of the Company as a whole. If after making and filing
our plans for future operations over a stated period the Minister,
in the exeroise of the power granted by the Regulations, increases
the amount we plan to import, we may be required to import quanti-
ties greatly in excess of our needs at a time when the prices which
we must pay for such products are most unfavorable, and if, on the
other hand, he reduces the amount we mey import, we may have avail-
able for sale quantities insufficient to meet the demands of our
business.
ADDITIONALCOMMENTS
In addition to the objections above set forth, which
refer to specifio provisions of the Control Law, Ordinance and
Regulations, there are other factors which we think should be
given serious consideration. We call particular attention to our
General Manager's letter of August 8, 1934 to the Minister of
Commeroe at Tokyo, copy of which was sent to the State Depart-
ment with our letter of August 30th, together with a summation
of known and probable objeotionable points to this law. As a
further attachment to our letter of August 30, we sent a copy of
a letter on the same subject written by the Managing Director of the
Rising Sun Petroleum Company, Ltd. (British interests) to the
Minister. Since then, in direct reply to this questionnaire pre-
pared by our representative, we have received a cable from Japan
dated September 5th, which reads:-
"Refer to our letter No. 671 P the following in effeot

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.99)
-8
summarises important points in official reply to our letter
of August 8th just received from Commerce Department ONE
Law recognizes established importers places no time limit
on continuance of their business and does not aim to obstruot
cont inuity Two Government when authorizing sales plans to
consider supply and demand and other things and also aims to
eliminate ruinous competition and states plans cannot be
sanotioned beyond 1 year THREE In view of increased con-
sumption government thinks it unlikely that our existing
sales will decrease unless under extraordinary circumstances.
SToP Refer to our telegram of Aug. 24th reduction of our
gasoline quota below average sales of previous 5 months due
clerical error by government we have requested rectification
which so far hes been refused. SToP Answer from Commerce
Department which we and friends consider inadequate and
evasive states government not yet in a position to officially
answer other questions and suggests that we interview under
officials which we both have decided not to do in view of
pending developments and as we consider verbal and unofficial
answers would serve no useful purpose SToP Have notified
respective Embassies STOp Japanese companies have suggested
renewing gasoline cartel which expired on August 3lst on
basis of quotas assigned by government but friends and our-
selves have declined to discuss quotas for the present however
to avoid unnecessarily weakening markets we both have stated
willing to continue price arrangements. Friends cabling
similarly."
We quote from our cable to Yokohama of September 7th as
follows:-
"Roferring to your cable of August 30th in view of pending
important developments affecting our business in Japan
particularly with respect to control law as well as other
considerations which may affect our future sources of
supply for Japan, we are definitely opposed to your filing
any application for refinery at this time STOP We therefore
think you should advise government in writing that in view
of their inability to give us any satisfactory assurance as
to our future in Japan as indioated by their reply to your
letter of Aug. 8th set forth in your cable of Sept. 5th, your
principals are unwilling to make definite application for
refinery at this time as huge additional expense involved
in no way warranted STOP However, this deoision is made in
full expectation government will not exolude from favorable
consideration at any future date our application to refine."
On September 13th, our Japan Office cabled that the
Dutoh Legation had been instructed to file a protest against stock
holding regulations and restriotion of gasoline quota, which

=== Page 24 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
information was passed on to our Embassy at Tokyo.
We are also attaching an extract from a letter from the
Managing Director of the Rising Sun Petroleum Company, Ltd. to
the Minister of Commeroe and Labor in which he asks assurances on
nine specific points, the answers to which, if obtainable, would
be of equal importance to us. We do not know the date of this
letter, but believe it was presented in the early part of August.
The seriousness of this entire mat ter cannot be too
greatly emphasized. Even prior to the enactment of the Petroleum
Industry Control Law, we were under Government control inasmuch
as prices on gasoline were concerned by virtue of the Industrial
Control Act, which was extended in November, l932 to cover gasoline,
and as pointed out in our memo of August 2l, our operations for the
year 1932 resulted in a loss of about 2,000,000 and in 1933 a
loss of about 300,O00. We can therefore see but little future
for our Company in Japan unless the onerous restrictions imposed
by the new law are removed.

()
soribed without regard to the amount of business done in

Mr. Hornbeok.

894.6363/96
Subjoot:
Japanese Petroleum Monopoly and
Related Developments and Problems.
Mr. Teagle called and inquired whether there were
any new developments in this case. I said that we had
had nothing new. I inquired whether he had anything.
He said that he had not but that he wondered whether we
had heard anything of action by the British Government.
I said that we had heard nothing since the information
which he had given us to the effect that the British
Foreign Office had instructed the British Ambassador in
Tokyo to confer with his American and his Dutoh colleagues.
I added that I was sure that if anything had oocurred the
Embassy in Tokyo would have informed us.
I then read to Mr. Teagle two paragraphs bearing on
the problem of American petroleum selling organizations
in China and in Japan from a letter from the Amerioan
Minister to China to me, of date August 23, in which Mr.
Johnson disoussed the problem of restrictive measures in
the
F/G

=== Page 31 === (confidence 0.96)
一 2 -
the United States for the purpose of inducing better treat-
ment in China and in Japan and the difficulties of getting
cooperation among the companies themselves and acquiescence
by them in governmental measures of control which might be
necessary to make this process effective.
From that point on the conversation covered the broad
range of problems which confront the American petroleum
industry, with regard to which some very interesting state-
ments of fact, of comment and of conjecture were made by
Mr. Teagle. At one point Mr. Teagle said that he had
been considering for some time the possibility that there
might be sent by him and by Sir Henri Deterding special
representatives to talk with high Japanese authorities, men
who could speak with more knowledge of the oil industry as
a whole and the politics of the oil business than can the
representatives of the companies who are the sales managers
in Japan. There was also a canvassing of various possibili-
ties of strategy or tactics. It was agreed that at the
moment there was probably no step which could to advantage
be taken either by Mr. Teagle or by the Government, and
that the attitude to be assumed for the time being should
be one of watchful waiting.

To G-2, Ninth Corps Area Hq.

Subject: Japanese Oil Situation.
Degertyient of State
1. From Mr. Galbraith, an employee of the Union Oil Co. of California,
am advised that the Japanese are buying record breaking supplies
of oil, and are now in the market with inquiries for large amounts
for delivery as of next March.
894.6363/97
It is suggested that more definite information might be had as to
quantities going to Japan from Dutch East India sources, by in-
quiry through attaches in Great Britain or in Holland. Not all
of the Japanese imports are coming from the United States, by any
means.
2.
Attached is translation of the oil law of Japan, previously referred
to. This law has been followed by the issuing of two Imperial Ordi-
nances laying down the procedure to be followed in detail.
The two outstanding purposes of the act are - l. To force firms
engaged in the oil industry in Japan to maintain a 50% surplus,
and 2. To enable the government to fix a price and buy all stocks
at that price with no further action, or delay - note paragraphs
SIX & SEVEN.
While under the act an individual firm is only required to main-
tain a surplus of 50% of its annual sales of oil, it has been
pointed out that in practice this will insure tankage of from a
year to a year and half supply. Note Importers must have tank-
age to hold 50% of their annual importations - Refiners must have
tankage to hold 50% of their annual refinery output, and dealers
who buy in part from local refiners and import in part will quite
FILED
likely be required to provide tankage for 50% of their total turn-
over.
This act in practice ties up 50% of the oil importers and refiners
inventory capital permanently. In addition he is faced with the
possibility of being ordered to increase the capacity of his plant
or tank farm, the Minister of Commerce being empowered to order
any increase he deems expedient in the interest of the public wel-
fare.
/s/
E. 0. Sawyer, Jr.,
Major - MI-Res.

SECRET

Dear Dr. Hornbeck:
Continuing with advices in respect to the
Petroleum Industry Control Law in Japan, we now have pleasure
in attaching in duplicate, two recent cables from Yokohama, namely,
dated October 10th and October 15th.
While it is probable that these require but
little explanation, nevertheless may we be permitted to comment as
follows:-
111
On the second page of the Cable of October 10th,
our Yokohama Office mention their questionnaire of August 8th. This
was addressed to the Minister of Commerce and Industry at Tokyo, and
a copy was forwarded you with our letter of August 30th. While per-
haps irrelevant at the time, Mr. Yoshino's assertion that the
Japanese Control Law is less burdensome than the French Law, is, in
our opinion, not in accordance with facts as determined by a pre-
vious comparison of the two, which unquestionably proved that the
former is far more onerous. This matter is again being reviewed
mainly for the purpose of advising Yokohama in just what respect
these two laws differ and when we write our Japan Office, we shall
take the liberty of sending you a copy of our remarks.
In regard to the Cable of October l5th, reference
on the second page to words 154 to 199 in Yokohama's message of
September 28th, is in respect to the question of the storage pro-
vision, which as you know, is one of the points to which we most
seriously object. If subsequent dispatches on this phase contain
information which may be of interest or value to you, we shall
pass it on to you.
Yours very truly,
Kemm4. bor

Dear Dr. Hornbeck:
Following our letter of October l7th, we
are taking the liberty of enclosing in duplicate, a cable
received today from our Yokohama Office.
The first part of this message deals with the
form we are obligated to submit to the Japanese authorities
covering the so-called Business Plan for 1935. When Yokohama
prepared this for the officials at Tokyo, they left blank that
part of the questionnaire in respect to complying with the storage
regulations of the Control Law, for obviously, they were not in a
position to submit such information, as the policy of the Company
covering this matter had not been formulated. In fact, this is
still the situation today. You will observe that at the suggest-
ion of the American Embassy our representatives in Japan do not
propose to reply at the moment.
If it is an appropriate request, we shall appre-
ciate it if we are notified of any developments in the dispatches
mailed by the Embassy and due in Washington next week, which in
your opinion, would be of interest to us. Further, if after re-
ceipt of these dispatches you think it advisable that someone
from our office proceed to Washington, we shall be much pleased
to make this arrangement.
Very truly yours,

Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
No. 984, dated September 21, 1934, and to previous corres-
pondence on the subject of American oil interests in Japan
and their difficulties under the new Petroleum Industry
Law. From recent indications it appears that the various
questions involved may soon reach an acute stage.
Representations to the Japanese Government.
In compliance with the Department's telegraphic instruc-
76
tion No. 162,/September 21, 8 p.m., I made oral represen-
tations to the Minister for Foreign Affairs on September
FLED
(C.

=== Page 49 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
25, 1934, reading to him that portion of the Department's
instruction which was intended for the Japanese Government.
At his request I left with him a copy of the representations
without, however, any indication that it was a written com-
munication from the Embassy to the Foreign Office. A copy
1/
of the document left with the Minister is enclosed herewith,
for the records of the Department. Mr. Hirota expressed
himself as sympathizing with the foreign oil companies in
their difficulties and expressed the hope that something
could be done in the premises. A copy of a memorandum of
my conversation with the Minister for Foreign Affairs is
2/
also enclosed.
The British Embassy at Tokyo has informed this Embassy
that it has received instructions from its Foreign Office to
make representations to the Japanese Foreign Office on the
subject of the Petroleum Control Law and its effect on
British oil interests, following the representations made
by the American Embassy. Mr. Sansom, the Commercial Coun-
selor of the British Embassy, informed a member of my staff
that, while his instructions did not authorize the Embassy
to ask for some modification of the present burdensome re-
gulations, in view of the fact that the American Embassy had
made such a request, he would include the request in the
representations which he was drafting. The representations
will be made in the course of the next few days, and the
Department will be informed by telegraph when they have
been filed with the Foreign Office.
The Stock-Holding Requirements.
A

=== Page 50 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
As was stated in the Embassy's despatch No. 984, of
September 21, 1934, the oil companies (native as well as
foreign) were required to file by September 30, 1934, with
the Department of Commerce and Industry, their plans for
1935, including their sales plans, plans for storage (to
include their plans for storage of the six months' stocks
required under the new law), and plans for importation of
the oil, both for working stocks and for storage. The for-
eign oil companies were unable to comply with the regulation,
as their head offices had not authorized them to consent to
store six months' stocks in Japan. At the same time, if
they failed to comply with the regulation, they made them-
selves liable to punishment under the law. They avoided the
difficulty temporarily by filing their sales plans only, and
asking for postponement of the time for filing the storage
and import plans, on the ground that these matters were the
subjects of discussion(referring here to the diplomatic repre-
sentations). A copy of the letter written to the Minister
for Commerce and Industry by the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company,
dated September 28, 1934, requesting the postponement, is
3/
enclosed for the information of the Department. The oil
companies hope that delaying the issue will give time for .
the diplomatic representations which have been made or which
will be made to take effect.
The question has arisen, however, of the next step should
the Department of Commerce and Industry refuse to grant the
extension of time and insist upon immediate filing of the
storage and import plans. According to the opinion of the
attorney

=== Page 51 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
attorney for the Rising Sun Petroleum Company, the filing
of the plans will constitute an obligation to fulfill
those plans should they be approved by the Department
of Commerce and Industry - a contingency which the for-
eign oil companies wish to avoid by every means in their
power. The two foreign oil companies have therefore in-
formally requested their respective Embassies to consider
the possibility of making representations on this point
on their behalf should the Department of Commerce and In-
dustry adopt an intransigeant attitude. It is therefore
possible that the Embassy will request instructions of
the Department in regard to this point in the near future.
Sales Quotas.
75
As was noted in the Embassy's despatch No. 957, dated
September 6, 1934, the quota for gasoline allotted to the
Standard-Vacuum Oil Company by the Department of Commerce
and Industry for the last half of 1934 was less than their
average sales in previous months and did not include any
part of the natural increase in demand, the natural in-
crease being entirely allotted to the Japanese refineries.
The Standard-Vacuum Oil Company protested the allotment,
but the Department of Commerce and Industry refused to ac-
> cept the protest. The Standard-Vacuum Oil Company eventually
accepted the allotment "against their will", but has addressed
another protest to the Department of Commerce and Industry,
giving in detail the causes for complaint against the allot-
ment, quoting previous assurances which had been made to the
effect that no discrimination would be practiced in the opera-
tion of the new law, and asking for reconsideration of the
guota

quota granted. A copy of this letter is enclosed herewith.
Mr. Goold, the General Manager for Japan of the Standard-
Vacuum Oil Company states that, if this latest protest
fails, he will request the Embassy to take up the matter
through diplomatic channels.

CLASSFIMSTION CD A thory: letter
STRIOTLY CONEIDENTIAL.
of 5:20-64   1. , Sa D
2NooleDale 3//67
I cannot escape the conclusion that every effort
should be made by our Government, in connection with the
petroleum situation in Japan and Manchuria, to protect
as far as may be possible the large interests of our
nationals in the oil trade of those regions, even at
the risk that such efforts may bring added irritation
to the relations between Japan and the United States.
The oil problem in Japan and Manchuria, which in-
dicates gross disregard of the rights and legitimate
interests of other nationals, is more than a mere matter
of the protection of commercial interests; it is a mat-
ter which directly concerns international policy and in-
ternational amity. Unfortunately, in all important in-
stances during recent years where the Japanese have dis-
played their power, force could not be used effectively for
the purpose of compelling a more reasonable attitude on
their part. In the case of Japan's oppression of the for-
eign oil interests, however, the nations concerned have
an effective remedy.
Petroleum is Japan's weakest point. The nation must
have considerable supplies of oil for its navy, merchant
marine, internal transport, air force, manufacturing in-
dustries and fishing fleets. The oil produced in Japan
mannot fill more than twenty per cent of the requirements.
By controlling the supply of crude oil at the sources, there-
fore, the interested countries may be able to induce a more
reasonable attitude on the part of the Government and the
people here toward other peoples and toward international
relations

=== Page 54 === (confidence 0.98)
- 7 -
relations in general.
From a purely commerical point of view, the action
indicated above would appear to be both appropriate and
feasible. The closing of the door to American oil com-
panies in Manchuria through the operation of a monopoly
system, in order to throw the business to a Japanese-
Manchurian refining company, and the probability that the
American oil companies selling refined products in Japan
will gradually be forced out of the Japanese market through
the quota system already established, would certainly ap-
pear to justify us in refusing longer to sell the Japanese
the materials with which they can accomplish these results.
By far the greatest part of the petroleum and petroleum
products used in Japan is of American origin. There would
seem to be no reason why an American raw material should
be supplied in unlimited quantities to assist in closing
the market to American finished products.
Moreover, the action of the Japanese Government in
compelling the foreign oil companies to pay for and store,
in tanks which they must themselves erect, large quantities
of oil in Japan in order to create a war reserve for Japan,
is one that can only be described as most unfair to the
foreign oil companies and one which should be resisted in
every way possible, even though the resistance may work a
temporary hardship on some interests, such as small inde-
pendent producing companies in California which occasionally
sell small quantities of crude oil to the Japanese refineries.
The
1

=== Page 55 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
The local managers of the two large foreign oil com-
panies operating in Japan are agreed that the plan of con-
trolling Japan's supply of crude oil would be commercially
feasible, provided that the Aerican Government could con-
trol exports from the United States. The Netherlands In-
dies fields are controlled by the Shell and Standard in-
terests; the Persian and Burman fields are controlled by
the Shell interests; the Iraq field is under the control
of an international company of which large shares are
held by the Shell and certain American interests. Other
sources available outside the United States are in Mexico,
Venezuela, Rumania and Russia. The Soviets are not ex-
pected to be able to supply Japan with large quantities
of crude oil, as their production is only slightly in ex-
cess of their own requirements; their crude oil, moreover
is not adapted to the existing Japanese refineries and the
cost of transportation from the Black Sea is excessive.
The Rumanian oil interests are anxious to sell crude oil
to Japan, but the quality of the oil is poor and transporta-
tion costs are again excessive. The Japanese hope to be
able to find sources of supply in Mexico and Venezuela,
but as yet the quantities available from independent pro-
ducers are small and the cost of transportation is heavy.
Moreover, such sources as Mexico, Venezuela, Rumania and
Russia would not be satisfactory in time of war, as the
lines of transportation are long and could be easily cut.
The managers of the foreign oil companies in Japan there-
fore believe that if the American Government could control
the

=== Page 56 === (confidence 0.98)
- 9 -
the export of crude oil to Japan from the United States,
the Shell and Standard interests would limit the export
from other fields controlled by them, and the Japanese
refineries would be placed in a most difficult position,
from which they could only extricate themselves by aban-
doning their plans for monopolizing the oil trade of
Japan and Manchuria.
Compelling the Japanese to abandon their plan of
building up large reserves of oil at the expense of the
oil companies would also appear to be advisable from a
military point of view. The Japanese military machine is
one of the most powerful in the world and therefore con-
stitutes a constant menace to the peace of the world. It
would seem, under the circumstances, to be inadvisable to
consent to any measures which would tend to strengthen this
machine.
893136334
In its telegram No. 182, August 20, 3 p.m., this Em-
bassy offered the suggestion that exports of crude oil to
Japan and Manchuria be limited or stopped, and further sug-
gested that, as an indication that some such measure was
being considered, the American Government might request ex-
porters of oil to furnish data regarding their exports, in
order to enable the Government to study the effects upon
our nationals' oil interests abroad of our exports of crude
oil. This suggestion was made in the belief that even a
threat of retaliatory measures would cause the Japanese
Government and oil interests to relax their pressure upon
American oil interests in Japan and Manchuria. It is be-
lieved that some initial step should be taken in the near
future, as the foreign oil companies are being forced into
an
A

=== Page 57 === (confidence 0.98)
- 10 -
an increasingly difficult position.
It is realized that many difficulties both domestic
and international are involved in attempting to control
the export of oil from the United States. It means in
many ways an acceptance of the concept that international
trade is a matter to be arranged by Governments; that the
individual merchant is to be directed, perhaps even sacri-
ficed, in an effort to promote what is supposed to be the
common good. Legal and perhaps political considerations,
of which the Embassy has no knowledge, might be involved
before a decision could be reached. For the foregoing
or other reasons it may not be feasible to adopt the sug-
gestions contained in this despatch, but at the present
writing, there se ms to be no other method of relieving
the situation here.
Respectfully yours,
Cam . truill
Edwin L. Neville
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim.

to despatch
 NO
...10p1 of ourobex s1934 from the
Embassy at Tokya
1. The American Government has noted the enactment
by the Japanese Government of a law (Law. No. 26 of March
27, l934) for the control of the petroleum industry
in Japan and, in view of the substantial participation
of American interests in that industry it is moved in all
friendliness to invite the attention of the Japanese
Government to the disabilities which the law under
reference threatens to impose upon American petroleum
interests in Japan.
2. The American firms concerned are convinced that
if as a condition to continuation of their business in
Japan they are to be required to install equipment and
maintain stocks of oil and oil products in excess of
their ordinary commercial requirements; if the required
stocks are to be subject to purchase by the Japanese
Government at a price to be fixed by that Government;
if there is likelihood or possibility that quotas will
be prescribed regardless of the amount of business done
in the past by firms that may be affected; and if there
is likelihood or possibility that quotas may subsequently
be raised or be lowered at will; -- these firms would
not be able to formulate long term plans for the reason-
able safeguarding of their enterprises in Japan and would
find it impossible to invest with any sense of security
funds necessary for the operation of those enterprises.
Accordingly it is felt that subjection of their enter-
prises to such conditions and the meeting by the companies
of such requirements would be tantamount to a laying upon
the companies of special burdens the effect of which
would be a contribution to a national objective of Japan
at heavy cost to the American enterprises concerned
but with no advantage or compensation to the enterprises
themselves.
3. In view of the destructive effect which appli-
cation of certain provisions of the law would presumably
have upon legitimate and established interests developed
over a period of many years by American enterprise in
response to economic needs of the Japanese people the
American Government expresses the hope that the Japanese
Government will see its way clear to avoid subjecting
American petroleum interests in Japan to the unusual
hazards and the burdensome restrictions upon the normal
conduct of business which are apparently implicit in the
law under reference.

Memorandum of Conversation between
The Minister for Foreign Affairs, Mr. Hirota,
and Mr. Neville, September 25, 1934.
Oil Situation
I called on the Minister by appointment at 3 o'clock.
I told him that I had come by direction of my Government
to deliver an oral communication in regard to the oil
situation. I read to him that portion of the Department's
telegraphic instruction No. 162, September 21, 8 p.m.,
which was intended for the Japanese Government. At his
request I left him a copy without any indication that it
was a written communication of any description from the
Embassy to the Foreign Office. He said that of course he
was not familiar with the details and that the matter was
still in the hands of the Department of Commerce and In-
dustry which was studying it very carefully. He said that
the Foreign Office recognized the hardship which would be
worked upon foreign companies particularly but upon
Japanese oil companies as well if they were required to
expend large sums for storage purposes and then had no as-
surance that they would be licensed to continue in busi-
ness, or if so, what amount of petroleum products they
would be permitted to sell. He said that the Foreign
Office was constantly bringing up this point for con-
sideration and he hoped that something could be done to
ease the situation. He said that of course Japan had to
import oil and probably would import it in increasing
quantities. For that reason it was desirable that the
process be not too complicated.
E.L.N.

October 3, 1934
His Excellency
Machida Chuji,
Minister of Commerce & Industry,
T o k y o
Your Excellency,
On September 18th, Mr. Yoshino was kind enough to
tell Mr. Tsuchiya of our Company that he understood we
were dissatisfied with the gasoline sales quota of 96,000
kilolftres assigned to us by the Government for the last
half of 1934 as stipulated in letters received from the
Commerce Department dated August 20th 9-Ko No. 920 and
September 26th 9-Ko Ko No. 766, and added that if our
Company considered it had reasonable grounds for dissatis-
faction, the Government would have no objection to our
frankly bringing our viewpoint to their attention.
In consequence, we summarize below the principal
reasons for our dissatisfaction. Although some of the
points have already been brought to the attention of the
Government in our letters to Your Excellency of August
24th, August 31st, September 7th, and September 13th,
they are repeated herein for easy reference.
Basic Period Used by Shokosho
In arriving at the quota assigned to our Company,
(as mentioned in our letter of August 3lst), the Govern-
ment informed representatives of the foreign oil companies
at an interview at the Shokosho on August 30th, that they
had used as a basis the Seven Company Gasoline Cartel
period of June, July, August 1934. The reasons given
by the Shohosho for adopting this basis were briefly as
follows. That although they originally had intended to
base sales quotas for the last half of 1934 on the actual
trade results of the first six months of 1934, they had
finally decided to use the sales quotas allotted under
the Seven Party Cartel; First because sales during the
months of June, July and August (i.e. the period of the
Seven Party Cartel) would be more indicative of present
consumption, and Second because these quotas had been
agreed to by the seven companies prior to the enforcement
of the Control Law.
We

=== Page 62 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
We do not consider it equitable or consistent to have
used the foregoing basis for the following reasons:
1. When the representatives of the Six Party Cartel
called at the Shokosho on May 31st, 1934, in response to
an invitation from Dr. Matsumoto, the foilowing transpired:
"The Conference Companies explained that
one of the difficulties in arriving at
acceptable quotas for a short period was
the fact that the Russians - in the hope
of securing an even larger quota when the
Law came into effect - were trying to obtain
now an abnormally large quota for the three
months temporary arrangement. It was there-
fore suggested in order to make it easier
to come to an arrangement with the Russians,
that the Department of Commerce and Industry
might be willing to decide now that when
fixing import licenses under the Control Law,
they would ignore entirely the quotas assigned
to the seven companies during any three months
agreement that might be concluded. Dr.
Matsumoto agreed to this latter suggestion,
stating that the Russians might be told that
this represented the formal decision of the
Government."
Notwithstanding this assurance which was conveyed
to the Russians and notwithstanding the fact that our
Company voluntarily restricted its sales during June, July,
and August in order to fall in line with the wishes of the
Government to stabilize the market and make effective more
normal selling prices for gasoline, the Shokosho not only
ignored but actually (as far as the foreign oil companies
are concerned) used this Seven Party Agreement period as
a basis for assigning quotas for the last half of 1934
which has worked out very much to the disadvantage of our
Company. The foregoing understanding may be confirmed
by reference to our letter to Dr. Matsumoto dated June lst,
1934, to which was attached Minutes of the Meeting of
May 3lst, from which the above quotation is extracted.
From the foregoing it is obvious, therefore, that the
quotas assigned to us under the Seven Party Agreement for
June, July, August 1934 do not represent an accurate cross
section of our business nor our normal rate of trade.
2. In the letter from the Shokosho dated August 20th,
9-Ko No. 920, when stating the reason for assigning us a
quota of 96,Oo0 kilolitres, it was explained that they
"considered this figure proper in view of our actual
sales prior to the enforcement of the Petroleum Industry
Controi Law and of the actual demand for gasoline in Japan".
However, our sales for the three months prior to the en-
forcement of the Control Law, i.e. during April, May and
June

=== Page 63 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
June, - as well as for the three months prior to the
Seven Party Agreement, i.e. March, April and May - were
in each instance greater than the quotas for the three
months of the Seven Party Agreement, 1.e. June, July and
August, 1934, which were used by the Shokosho. There-
fore, it is obvious that the quota assigned to us not
only does not represent our rate of trade prior to the
enforcement of the Control Law, but also does not permit
us to share in the natural increase in consumption.
This, notwithstanding the very definite assurances from
the Minister of Commerce and others in answer to our
letter to His Excellency dated February 12th, 1934, that
we need have no cause for anxiety regarding the effect
upon our business of the Petroleum Industry Control Law,
the enactment of which was then under consideration.
Basic Sales Figures Used by Shokosho
When we inquired in our letter of August 24th,
concerning the mathematical basis used in arriving at our
gasoline sales quota of 96,000 kilolitres, representatives
of the foreign oil companies, in response to an invitation
to call on the Shokosho, were told that the Government
had used as a basis the Seven Company Cartel quotas for
June, July and August, and that to arrive at our quota
for the last half of i934 had converted these quotas to
kilolitres.and doubled the result, rounded off to the
nearest 1,Oo0 kilolitres. However, as explained in our
letter of August 3lst, 1934, to Your Excellency, had the
quotas actually assigned to us by the Conference been
used, we would have been entitled to sell during the last
six months of 1934, 97,030 kilolitres as the figures
below will illustrate:

The comments we desire to make in regard to the basic
figures used by the Government are as follows:
1. As shown above, the basis used by the
Shokosho results in our being penalized 1,030
kilolitres as compared with what we were en-

been used. This, notwithstanding the fact
that

siderably below our normal volume.
2. Inasmuch as the Shokosho decided to
use a three months period as a basis for
arriving at sales quotas for the Foreign Oil
Companies, it would in our opinion have been
more consistent and equitable, particularly in
view of Dr. Matsumoto's previous assurances
referred to in Page 2, to have taken the sales
for the three months immediately preceding the

3. We were informed by the Shokosho that
the figures they used were obtained from two
Japanese Oil companies in the Cartel. We do
not understand why our figures were not
requested officially from the Cartel itself
or obtained direct from our Company either
of which methods would have insured accuracy.
4. The net result is that we have been
deprived not only of any participation in the
growing gasoline trade, made available by the
natural increase in consumption, which has been
assigned entirely to the Japanese as well as a
semi-Japanese company, but our business has
actually been cut below our average monthly
rate of trade during the past six months. The
situation in which we find ourselves is further
aggravated by the fact that we were not notified
by the Government of our reduced allotment for
the last half of 1934 until we received from
the Shokosho, letter 9-Ko No. 920 dated
August 20th.' In consequence, greater sales
restrictions, in order to adjust our position,
would

=== Page 65 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
would have to be concentrated in the relatively
short period of four months, viz: September to
December 1934. This is already causing concern
to Mitsui as well as to our Japanese selling
agents, the livelihood of many of the latter
being dependent upon their business connection
with us.
5. We believe it will be difficult, if not
impossible, for some of the companies to deliver
the quotas allotted to them by the Government.
Furthermore, we desire to point out that experience
shows that if any one company attempts to in-
crease its business too rapidly, as the Japanese
Companies will have to do if they sell their
greatly increased quotas, selling prices in-
variably weaken and an unstable market situation
develops. This is what is happening today.
Summary
Summarized briefly, the principal grounds for our
dissatisfaction are as follows:
A.
Our gasoline business has been reduced to a
level below that at which we were trading
before the enforcement of the Control Law.
B.
We have not been allowed to continue to par-
ticipate in the growing business resulting from
the natural increase in gasoline consumption.
C.
By falling in line with the wishes of the
Government to reach an agreement with Matsukata
(to accomplish which our Company was primarily
instrumental) and voluntarily restricting our
business for June, July and August 1934, our
trade has been penalized for the last half of
1934, notwithstanding prior assurances from the
Government to the contrary and also assurances
from the Government that we had no cause for
fear in regard to the administration of the
Petroleum Control Law.
D.
We consider the basis used by the Government in
arriving at our quota was inequitable first
because of the period used and second because
the quotas used by the Government did not
represent our actual trade during the period
used, whereas, had our actual rate of trade
prior to the Seven Party Agreement been used
as a basis, our quota would have been about
3,000 kilolitres larger.
E

=== Page 66 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
E.
The reduced quota allotted us has caused great
concern to our New York Office and to Mitsui
as well as to our entire Japanese selling
organization.
Conclusion
We frankly feel that due consideration should be
given to the facts herein recited, all of which can be
substantiated. In view of the very serious and far-
reaching effect on our business and upon our Japanese
selling organization which the restriction imposed on our
gasoline trade is having, we desire once again to formally
ask for reconsideration and adjustment in our position.
While in no way seeking to question the authority of the
Government under the Law to fix whatever business plans
it deems fit, at the same time we wish to express the hope
that we may be accorded treatment in line with the known
desire of the Japanese Government to administer the
Petroleum Control Law justly and impartially. It is
our firm belief that our Company, which has been in
business in Japan for over forty years, will be accorded
such treatment, when the facts are clearly recognized,
that has impelled us to again bring our point of view
to Your Excellency's attention.
We are, with all respect,
Your Excellency's most obedient servants,
STANDARD-VACUUM OIL COMPANY,
Representative.

Conversation.
October 24, 1934.
74.6363
Standard Oil Company of Now

OCT 2 5 1934
Mr. Hornbeok.
WESTERN EHROPEAN AFF
L.. S UF
Related Developments and Problems
894.6363/104
RECEIVED
T2 1934
Reference, Tokyo's telegram 232, October 22, 5 p.m.;
OFFICE OF THE SECRETARY
Tokyo's mail despatch lo0l, October 5, entitled "American
Oil Interests in Japan"; and Peiping's telegram 478,
October 21, 6 p.m.
Mr. Teagle called by appointment. Mr. Hornbeck said
that he was very glad to see Mr. Teagle, as we have just
received information regarding new developments -- discourag-
ing information -- with regard to, first, the Japanese
petroleum law and, second, the projeoted petroleum monopoly
1n Manohuria. Mr. Teagle replied that he also had received,
last evening, from New York, a substantial amount of new
information and that his Mr. Parker was sending what they
had to the Department. Mr. Teagle took from his pocket a
file which he said he supposed contained the same informa-
tion that we had.

=== Page 73 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
Mr. Hornbeok then said that it was evident that the
Japanese authorities are planning to go ahead regardless
of the representations which the interested oil companies
and the governments concerned have made. He reoalled the
prediction which had been made on the occasion of the
first call of Mr. Teagle and Sir Henri Deterding at the
-4
Department in connection with this matter, that representa-
tions would be of little avail unless it was made evident
to the Japanese that disregard of the representations would
be likely to result in embarrassment to the Japanese in some
concrete form. He said that the demands which the Japanese
Government has made for early compliance by the foreign
petroleum companies with provisions of the law and the
regulations apparently makes it clear that the companies
must decide at an early date upon the course of action
which they will tell the Japanese Government they intend
to follow. He said that we had a memorandum prepared by
an officer of the Embassy in Tokyo -- which we might
conjecture was based in part at least upon a brief supplied
by the general manager for Japan of the Standard Vacuum
Oil Company -- in which it is pointed out that in relation
to supplies of crude oil Japan is very vulnerable; that
the major portion of the petroleum and petroleum products
now imported into Japan is of American origin; and that
"There would seem to be no reason why an American raw
material should be supplied in unlimited quantities to
assist

=== Page 74 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
assist in closing the market to American finished
produots."
There ensued some discussion of commercial factors,
in the course of which Mr. Teagle referred to the sug-
gestion which he had originally made to the Department
that the American Government take steps toward regulating
(restricting or embargoing) exports of oil from the United
States to Japan. Mr. Hornbeck said that, speaking un-
officially and purely on the basis of a personal impression,
he doubted very much whether it could be expected that the
Government would take steps in that direction. He said
that he had studied the utteranoes and the acts of the
highest officers of the Administration and that it was
his impression that the thought of those officers did not
run in that direction; he might be mistaken, he was speak-
ing not on the basis of any decision of which he had
knowledge or of anything said to him but on the basis of
an impression; he felt that this subject was one with
regard to which it would be well for Mr. Teagle to talk
with the highest authorities; in fact, he felt that the
whole problem under discussion should be taken up by
Mr. Teagle with the Under Secretary or the Secretary of
State -- the problems involved being problems of very
substantial importance both to the American oil interests
involved and the various governments concerned. Mr. Teagle
said

=== Page 75 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 -
said that he concurred in the view that the matter should
be treated as one of unusual importance and that he would
expeot to discuss it with higher officers, but that at
this moment the highest officer of the Standard Vacuum
Company is in London and is waiting to confer with Sir Henri
Deterding at a meeting which will take place probably early
next week; he, Mr. Teagle, therefore would not be in posi-
tion to do anything until after that conference. Mr.
Hornbeck said that he was glad to hear that such a conference
was scheduled to occur, and that it seemed to him that the
oil interests should decide upon a course of action for
themselves which would not be contingent upon further
governmental action: that is, that they should decide
whether they intend to comply with the Japanese law and
regulations or to tell the Japanese that they cannot and
will not comply therewith. Mr. Teagle inquired what would
be the result of non-compliance. Mr. Hornbeok replied that
it would probably depend in considerable measure on the
manner in which the petroleum interests handled the matter
in giving notice of such intent -- and on the manner in
which they "followed up"; it might be that the Japanese
would offer some kind of a compromise; it might be that
they would endeavor to buy the equipment which the companies
have in Japan and thereafter to handle petroleum business
in Japan themselves. Mr. Teagle asked what there was to
prevent

=== Page 76 === (confidence 0.99)
-5 -
prevent an automatio confiscation by the Japanese Govern-
ment of this equipment. Mr. Hornbeok said that he did
not believe that that question need arise: if it became
clearly evident to the Japanese that the companies were
prepared to refuse to do business on the conditions thus
far laid down by the Japanese and also evident that, in
the event of their being frozen out of Japan, the companies
would exert themselves toward drying up the sources of
petroleum imports to Japan and to Manchuria, the Japanese
would probably think very seriously before taking the
responsibility of creating such an impasse. Mr. Teagle
then referred to the difficulty of bringing independent
oil producers into line. Mr. Hornbeck said that he
realized that this was a very real problem and he felt
it was one to which the industry should address its best
thought and effort.
At this point Mr. Hornbeck asked that he be permitted
to introduce Mr. Mackay -- and Mr. Maokay was called in.
Mr. Hornbeok informed Mr. Mackay that he had suggested to
Mr. Teagle that the oil companies come to a decision with
regard to the course of action which they would pursue
vis-à-vis the Japanese authorities; he said that he had
by implication suggested that the companies prepare for a
contest; that he believed that the Japanese Government,
if it saw that the great producers of petroleum were pre-
pared

=== Page 77 === (confidence 0.97)
- 6-
pared to exert themselves toward refusal to supply orude
petroleum to Japan and that the governments concerned
were at least sympathetically disposed toward that effort,
would hesitate to force the issue. Mr. Mackay indicated
concurrence in that view. Mr. Teagle then said that we
might all await with interest the report which would be
made to him sometime next week of the conference scheduled
to be held in London to which he had earlier referred.
Mr. Hornbeck said that we would all continue to give the
question our thought. The conversation there ended.

Tokyo's telegram No. 24l of
November 1, 6 p.m., makes available to
the Department the substance of an in-
formal memorandum from the Foreign Of-
fice in reply to the Embassy's oral
representations of September 25 in re-
gard to the Japanese Petroleum Industry
Law (see Department's telegram No. 162
of September 21, 8 p.m.). As "similar
memoranda' were received by the British
Embassy and the Dutoh Legation it would
appear that the Japanese Government has
in this manner replied to the various
representations made to it by the three
prinoipally interested governments.
The memorandum of the Foreign
Office would appear to state more
clearly and conoisely than hitherto the
views of the Japanese Government in re-
gard to certain phases of the question
under discussion. It does not, however,
appear to contain any concessions of
importance to the foreign oil companies
and it is significant that no mention
whatever is made of the all-important
requirement

=== Page 79 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
requirement of the Law in regard to addi-
tional oil storage. To be sure, assur-
anoe is given that the quotas of the
foreign oil companies will not be cur-
tailed below the limits of their business
prior to the enforcement of the Law but
this holds true only as long as the de-
mand for petroleum products in Japan con-
tinues to inorease.
Another item savoring of a concession
is the statement that the Government has
no intention of taking over at other than
market prices oil owned by the foreign
companies. In the same breath, however,
the Government reserves the right to con-
trol prices if the public interest demands.
It is believed that the representa-
tives in Tokyo of the American, British
and Dutch Governments will immediately ac-
quaint the concerned oil companies with
the views of the Japanese Government as
contained in the memorandum under refer-
ence and that the companies will, without
delay, relay the information to their
respeotive head offices together with
interpretive and suggestive comment. Thus
for the present it is suggested that no
action be taken by the Department in regard
to Tokyo's telegram under reference.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
101
No. 1001, dated October 5, 1934, and to previous corres-
pondence on the subject of American oil interests in Japan
and their difficulties under the new Petroleum Industry
Law.
Representations.
FIHED
As reported in the Embassy's telegram No. 222, Ootobe
10, noon, the British Ambassador to Japan called at the
Foreign Office on October 5, 1934, and made renewed re-
presentations on behalf of the British oil interests op-
erating

=== Page 89 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
erating in Japan. The Ambassador left with the Vice
Minister for Foreign Affairs an aide memoire, expressing
the "hope that it will be found possible to direct or
modify the operation of the Law in such a way as to re-
lieve them (the British oil interests) of hardships
which it threatens to impose". A copy of the aide me-
1/
moire referred to is enclosed herewith.
The Embassy has been informe that a written reply
to the British representations is being prepared by the
Foreign Office. An oral reply to the American oral re-
presentations will prob bly be made at the same time
that the reply to the British representations is delivered.
Oil Storage Requirements.
As was stated in the Embassy's despatch No. 100l, of
October 5, 1934, the two foreign oil companies did not
comply with the requirements that by September 30, 1934,
they file with the Minister for Commerce and Industry
their sales, storage and import plans for the year 1935.
They submitted their sales plans only, requesting a post-
ponement of the time for filing their import and storage
plans, on the ground that these matters were the subject
of discussion. The Department of Commerce and Industry,
however, has refused to grant an extension of the time for
filing these plans and under date of October 13, 1934,
demanded the submission of the complete business plan. I
21
enclose herewith a translation of the letter which that
Department sent to the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company. A
similar letter was sent to the Rising Sun Petroleum Com-
pany (British).
The

=== Page 90 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
The Standard-Vacuum Oil Company is therefore placed
in a most difficult position. Refusal to comply with the
demand of the Department of Commerce and Industry renders
the Company subject to cancelation of its license to oper-
ate in Japan and to a fine of Yen 30o0. At the same time
it cannot submit storage and import plans, as to do so
would commit it to the fulfillment of plans which the
Company cannot see its way (at present, at least) to carry
out.
Mr. Dickover, of the staff of the Embassy, therefore
called upon Mr. Kurusu, the Chief of the Bureau of Com-
mercial Affairs of the Foreign Office, on October 18, 1934,
in an attempt to discover the official view of the probable
fat of the foreign oil companies should they continue to
avoid complying with the stock-holding provisions of the
Petroleum Industry Law. Mr. Kurusu, unfortunately, was
unable or unwilling to throw much light on the subject. A
memorandum of the conversation between Mr. Dickover and Mr.
31
Kurusu is enclosed herewith.
It would seem that the only course remaining to the
foreign oil interests in Japan is to avoid submitting
the storage and import plans demanded by the Department of
Commerce and Industry. It will then be necessary for the
Japanese Government to take the next step. If that step
is adverse to the American and British oil interests in
Japan, perhaps the Department will be in a position to con-
sider the implementing of the suggestions contained in the
Strictly Confidential section of the Embassy's despatch
101
No. 1001,lof October 5, 1934, and in the Embassy's
telegram

Respectfully yours,

British Embassy,
Tokyo.
5th October, 1934.
Aide-Memoire
His Majesty's Ambasaador has the honour to refer to
his conversation with the Vice-Minister for Foreign
Affairs on July 26th concerning the anxiety felt by the
British petroleum interests operating in Japan as to their
future position under the Petroleum Industry Law.
His Majesty's Ambassador learns from the represent-
atives of those interests that, although they have on
more than one occasion been courteously received by the
competent authorities, they have not so far been able to
obtain assurances of a nature to relieve their anxiety or
information to enable them to reach a decision as to their
future business policy. Consequently they have found
themselves in the embarrassing position of being unable
fully to comply with provisions of the Law which require
them to furnish by a fixed date deteils of their plans for
the year 1935.
In these circumstances His Majesty's Ambassador begs
leave to enquire whether the Japanese Government have been
able to give consideration to the position of the British
petroleum interests as it is effected by the Petroleum
Industry Law and to express on behalf of His Majesty's
Government the hope that it will be found possible to
direct or modify the operation of that Law in such a way
as to relieve them of hardships which it threatens to
impose.

No 1017of 00.19, 1934 from the
Tu hnssy at Tokyo.
Memorandum of Conversation between
Mr. Dickover and Mr. S. Kurusu, Chief
of the Bureau of Commercial Affairs,
Foreign Office, October 18, 1934.
I called on Mr. Kurusu at the Foreign Office at
12.15 p.m., and said that I ha not come to make any re-
presentations of any kind, but merely wanted to talk over
the oil situation with him. He said that he would be glad
to talk things over, and that the Foreign Office appreciated
very much the fact that the American Embass had not made
a formal diplomatic issue of the oil problem, but instead
had treated the matter orally and informally. I asked
if a reply would be given to our oral representations. He
said that, if one is desired, an oral reply will be made.
I said that I thought that we would appreciate a reply.
I then said that it seemed to me that the problem
of the position of the foreign oil companies in Japan had
reached a stalemate. The Minister for Commerce and Indus-
try had written the oil companies to send in their plans
for importing and storing oil, and for building storage
facilities, as was required by the new Petroleum Industry
Law. They had previously sent in their sales plans for
next year, but had purposely not filled in that part of the
form devoted to their storage and import plans, as to do
so would have committed them to fulfillment of those plans,
while they were not yet authorized by their head offices
to build tanks and store oil in Japan. I said that of
course I could not know what the oil companies would decide
to do in the future, but at present they certainly were not
inclined

=== Page 95 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
inclined to invest further large sums in Japan, when
the future of their business here is so uncertain.
I asked him what was going to happen if the foreign oil
companies continued to refuse to comply with the stock-
holding provisions of the law.
Mr. Kurusu said that the matter was puzzling him
also. Several things might happen. One was to consider
a part of the existing storage facilities of the foreign
oil companies as devoted to oil storage in accordance with
the law, and to allot sales quotas to the companies in
that proportion. I said that that plan would cut the quo-
tas of the foreign oil companies by about two-thirds, which
would create a gasoline famine in Japan, as the Japanese
refineries were not yet equipped to handle all of the de-
mand. Mr. Kurusu agreed, and said that it would be im-
possible to allot the difference to the Japanese companies.
For example, he said, let us suppose that the Standard Oil
is selling 300, and some Japanese company 100. We can cut
the Standard Oil to 100, and give the difference of 200 to
the Japanese company, which would give it an allotment of
300. But the Japanese company is already having enough
trouble trying to finance its storage facilities for its
original l0o, and could not possibly arrange to store for
a sales quota of 300.
Another possibility was that the law might be modified.
He said that he imagined that the French and Spanish govern-
ments started out with some such proposition as the present
Japanese law, but found that it was unworkable and had to
modify

=== Page 96 === (confidence 0.98)
-3 -
modify it. I pointed out that the French law was much
less objectionable, as it required only three months'
stocks, whic could include working stocks, while the
Japanese law required six months' stocks, in addition
to working stocks. Mr. Kurusu agreed, and said that he
did not think that the Japanese law was workable. He
said that he had told "those chaps" (presumably the of-
ficials of the Department of Commerce and Industry) that
their whole idea was a "pipe dream" and would not work,
but that they would not listen to him and had "put it
over".
And then, he said, there was the possibility that the
foreign oil companies might give it all up as a bad job
and quit Japan. But, he said, they could not very well
do that,as to do so would mean that they would lose most of
their present investments in Japan. I agreed, and said
that those investments amounted to a considerable sum,
over fifty million yen. Moreover, I said, their quitting
Japan would create a lot of trouble, as they employed, at
a guess, some twenty or thirty thousand people, who would
be thrown out of work. Also, I pointed out, their quitting
Japan would not remedy the situation as far as the law is
concerned, as no one is prepared to take up their business
and store the amount of oil which they are required to
store.
Mr. Kurusu then asked me if I thought it possible
that the foreign oil companies would consent eventually to
comply with the stock-holding requirements of the law. I
sa1d

=== Page 97 === (confidence 0.96)
- 4 -
said that I did not think it commercially feasible for
them to do so. If the Standard interests, for example,
invested ten million yen more in Japan, to provide storage
facilities and fill them with oil, they would have to make
an additional profit of five or six hundred thousand yen
annually to cover the interest on and amortization of
their investment. But they were prevented, under the law,
from making this additional profit, because they would not
be allowed to participate in the natural increase in the
demand for petroleum products, and they could not raise
their prices, which were fixed by the Government. Mr.
Kurusu agreed entirely, and said that he had been preach-
ing this to "those chaps" for months, but they knew nothing
of business and could not see the point.
I then said that, while the Japanese oil companies
might consider it a patriotic duty to carry large stocks
of oil at their owm expense for purposes of national de-
fense, the Japanese Government could hardly expect the
foreign oil companies to carry Japan's war-time reserves of
oil. Mr. Kurusu agreed, and said that, in his opinion,
it was up to the Government to pay for its own reserves
of oil and not to expect private concerns,Japanese or
foreign, to carry them at their own expense. But, he said,
in the long run it will be the public, and not the oil
companies, which will have to bear the burden. The oil
How,1
companies will have to raise their prices or go bankrupt,
the
if they have to store six months' supplies, and so eventually
2e5
the public will be compelled to pay for the storage. Then
pics
the
they

=== Page 98 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
they will realize, said Mr. Kurusu, that "something had
been put over on them" and there will be trouble with
the bus companies and the taxi companies.
Mr. Kurusu then said that the law was a bad one all
around and was bound to create trouble, either internationally
or internally, if "those chaps" insisted on enforcing it.
I said that it appeared that he was unable to tell me
more about what was liable to happen to the foreign oil
companies than I already knew. Mr. Kurusu said that that
was the case. He then called up Mr. Yoshino, the Vice
Minister for Commerce and Industry, and asked him about the
latest developments. According to Mr. Kurusu, Mr. Yoshino
told him that he had explained the situation to Mr. Goold,
and that Mr. Goold had sent a long telegram to his head of-
fice. They were now waiting for a reply to this telegram.
I said that I had seen the telegram, but that I did not re-
member that it called for a reply.
E.R.D.
