Mr. Teagle called me on the telephone from New York
and inquired whether I had received his air mail letter
of October 25.
I replied that the letter had reached me this
morning. Mr. Teagle asked what it might be possible for
him to say to his associates in London. I said that
first of all that I would like to inquire what he thought
of the stories which had appeared in the press today and
yesterday. Mr. Teagle replied that he found them very
interesting and he wondered where the correspondents got
them. I said that as far as my knowledge went the first
stories had come from Tokyo. Mr. Teagle said that that
was his impression. I said that this seemed to be in
ED
line with the suggestion which the representatives of the
oil interests in Japan had made, namely, that there should
be publicity. Mr. Teagle replied: Yes. Mr. Teagle then
reverted
F/G

=== Page 103 === (confidence 0.98)
一 2 -
reverted to the original question: what might he say
(he said "telegraph) to his London associates. I suggested
that he might find it more expedient to telephone them than
to telegraph them. He replied that it would be simpler
for him to telegraph and that he was perfectly confident
of the inviolability of his codes. I then said that he
would remember and he might remind his associates that
we had taken the position at the time of Mr. Teagle's and
Sir Henri Deterding's call that we would be prepared to
consider sympathetically suggestions, if and when, which
might come to us from the British or the Netherland Govern-
ments for cooperative action; and that that was still our
attitude. Mr. Teagle then said: But what about a joint
protest. I replied that I thought the word "protest"
should be ruled out. Mr. Teagle hastily said that he
meant "representations". I replied that we would like
to see what the British Government might choose to suggest.
I added that I thought I should in all candor point out
that the matter would require little time for considera-
tion; Mr. Teagle and I had both been of the opinion in
the course of our conversation of October 24 that no
further action need be taken until Mr. Teagle had reported
to me next week on the conference which was to be held
between representatives of the oil interests in London
early nert week; that if Mr. Teagle's associates in London
told the British Foreign Office of our attitude, it would
probably

=== Page 104 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
probably be a matter of some days before the Foreign
Office formulated an approaoh, if and when, to us on
the subjeot; that we did not want -- and we could assume
that the British Foreign Office would not want -- to
inject into relations with Japan at an inopportune moment
matters which might get in the way of the effort which is
being made at London in the preliminary naval conversations;
so, it should not be expected that the governments concerned
would rush precipitately into further action with regard to
the petroleum issues; and, it must be remembered, we had
taken action recently and we want to have some indication
of the position which the interested companies themselves
intend to take. Mr. Teagle said that he understood and
that he would forthwith inform his London associates of
our position. He added that he would call me on the
telephone early nert week. The conversation there ended.

During attendance at the National Foreign Trade Con-
vention, I communicated with Mr. Teagle by telephone and
at his request called on him and talked over the latest
developments with re ard to the Japanese petroleum law
and the projected petroleum monopoly in Manchuria. Mr.
2
Teagle read to me cables which he had, subsequent to
our long-distance telephone conversation of November 29,
sent to his correspondents in London. He had informed
those oorrespondents that he felt it imperatively necessary
that the companies come to an understanding with regard
to a common program and that the American Government was
still awaiting an initiative from the British Government.
Mr. Teagle asked me whether I thought of anything else
FTED

correspondents. He said that he was expecting a reply

not be appropriate for me to offer advice on such a
matter, but that I could say that I felt strongly that
the companies should come to a decision among themselves
with regard to a course or courses of action which they
would follow, given various contingencies and eventualities,

plans and intentions in that connection.

SKH
MEMORANDUM
We quote from cable received from Mr. O.C. Harden
to Messrs. Teagle and Brown:
"LONDON - November 1, 1934.
"We fully recognize negotiation Japanese Government must
be conducted by our own representatives  Stop However
so that Japanese may assume reasonable attitude and
give representatives of oil companies fair treatment,
feel it essential joint diplomatic representations
precede conference oil companies.
"Are working on principle of acceptable settlement which
should be completed next Monday.
"Agnew fully discussed matter with foreign office who
are working on problem and he expects definite informa-
tion early next week - Stop Holland also waiting on
British.
"Godber sailing SS: Ranchi November 16 for Singapore
due there December 7 prepared to continue to Japan
with authority to deal in matter.
"G. S. Walden sailing Conti Verdi November 9 arriving
Singapore November 27 and will proceed by same steamer
to Japan arriving December 5 - STOP Feel Parker should
plan to arrive about same time.
"Agnew strongly feels diplomatic action should not be
jeopardized by premature offer by companies He be-
lieves Japanese are endeavoring to drive wedge between
major foreign concerns and that joint diplomatic action
will destroy their hopes in this direction."

Dear Dr. Hornbeck:
Detartrornut of State
894.6363/11
I very much regret that circumstances did not
permit of my mailing you last Friday a copy of the attached
telegram from Yokohama, dated November 2nd. However, we con-
sider there is nothing in it that requires particular action
and that it was sent simply to keep us advised of the latest
developments.
There are one or two points in the message which
perhaps require elaboration, namely, our representations to
the Minister of Commerce and Industry of August 3lst, were specific
3
in that we stated we were cofinitely opposed to the huge additional
financial outlay required by the stock holding regulation, and im-
plied that we were only willing to comply with this regulation
provided the Japanese Government furnished the money both in re-
speot to additional construction necessary and to cover the cost of
the reserve stock. Further, we stated that we felt the price for
such stocks should be fixed before shipment and not based on the
market price in Japan when and if taken over.
The reference to words 154 to 214 in Yokohama's
message of Sept. 28th, deals with the rendering of the business
form for l935, wnich with the concurrence of our Embassy, was filed
incomplete with the authorities as it omitted information in regard
to carrying out reserve stock requirements.
In respect to the copy of the message we had pleasure
in handing you last Friday, purely or the sake of ready reference
may we again state that Messrs. Agnew and Godber are Directors of
Shell interests, while, as you no doubt recollect, Mr. G.S.Walden,
also mentioned in that message, is Chairman of our Board.
Yours very truly,
MermyI. Gor
KFC:M
Kersey F. Coe.

Dear Dr. Hornbeck:
We quote in its entirety cable dated November
7th received today from Yokohama:
"In addition to points named in our telegram of Nov.2nd
we interpret substance of reply to Sept. 25th representa-
tions as follows - FIRST Aithough plans are sanctioned
annually for only 1 year in advance our trade will not
be restricted below that done prior to enforcement of
law as long as demand continues to increase - 
SECOND
Owing to various factors it is impossible to estimate
and distribute increase in the demand beyond 1 year in
advance but as a policy Japan desires to refine rather
than import finished products therefore idle refining
capacity will have priority and the balance of the in-
crease in the demand will be distributed fairly among
all refiners and importers - THIRD It will be impossible
for the Petroleum Committee to act on application to
refine unless accompanied by concrete plan but except in
special circumstances it will be comparatively easy for
importers to obtain permission to refine if within import
quotas previously assigned by government - FOURTH -
It
will be especially easy for importers to obtain permission
to refine if the enterprises include more than 50% Japanese
capital - FIFTH - Government will not interfere with sell-
ing price of petroleum without good reason and if in any
emergency government purchase reserve supply under Article
No. 6 of the law it will be at current price - FULLSTOP
Refer to our telegram of Oct. l9th friends and ourselves
have received official communication from Commerce Depart-
ment dated November 5th demanding completion of form No. 4
by Nov. 15th and warning us if we fail that the smooth
execution of our l935 business plan might be interfered
with STOP Friends also American British Dutch Embassies
with whom we are consulting are notifying their principals
regarding this communication and we will telegraph again
tomorrow FULLSTOP Reply dated Nov. 5th from Foreign Office
F/G
records American and British representations regarding

Subject:
Japanese Petroleum Monopoly and
Related Developments and Problems.
RECEIVED
Mr. Parker called me on the telephone from New York.
He inquired whether there was anything new since the
telephone conversation between Mr. Teagle and me of
6
yesterday. I said that there was nothing new. Mr. Parker
said that this would presumably be his last inquiry before
starting for Tokyo; he would like to know whether there is
any possibility that the American Government would be dis-
posed to do anything in the way of placing an embargo or
restriction upon export of petroleum. I said that we had
gone over that matter pretty thoroughly with Mr. Teagle,
and that, on the last occasion when the matter had been
discussed between Mr. Teagle and me, I had said to Mr.
Teagle, unofficially and as a matter of personal opinion,
that, upon the basis of my knowledge of the general thought
of the Administration, but without this question having
been put definitely and been subjected to a decision, it
FOLED
was my impression that action in that direction could not
be expected; and I had suggested that if the American
F/G
companies

=== Page 145 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
companies want a decision with regard to the point they
should themselves put the question up to the highest
authorities. I reminded Mr. Parker that it has been
indicated repeatedly in our statements during the con-
versations on this sub ject that the American Government
awaits an initiative by the British Government and we
definitely do not intend to "get out in front" in this con-
nection. Mr. Parker said that he understood this. He said
that his representative in Tokyo seemed still to think that
we might lead off with restrictions. I said that our
Ambassador in Tokyo has been informed of our attitude and
I have not the slightest doubt but that he has informed
the Standard Vacuum representative there.
I then went on to say that, from the fact that the
Dutch Shell people are sending representatives to Tokyo,
just as Mr. Parker is going there on behalf of Standard
Vacuum, we may assume that the British Government has given
them its blessing in that conneotion; and that presumably
the Ambassadors of all three countries at Tokyo will
assist these representatives of the companies toward
making contacts and going into conference with the Japanese
authorities. I did not see that more could be expected of
us at the present moment. Mr. Parker said that he felt
that we had been and were being very helpful. He wondered
whether I had any other suggestions. I said that I had
one: speaking unofficially and informally and personally,
工

=== Page 146 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
I felt that, confronted by a problem such as Mr. Parker
will be dealing with on his trip, those who are handling
the problem should seriously consider indications of
trends in the field of foreign trade and merchandising
abroad; that, more and more, governments are going to be
confronted with the question of employment for their own
people; that in countries circumstanced as are Japan and
China -- to say nothing of others --, there will presumably
be more and more a tendency to try to substitute domestic
labor and employment for foreign labor and imported services;
that, to limit the thought to Japan, the Japanese would
import raw materials but as far as possible do their own
processing and there own merchandising; hence, foreign
companies doing business in Japan would need to think
seriously before adding to their investments and expanding
their agencies of distribution within the country. No
one could say that there might not come about changes
in trend, both international and national, -- but the
trends of the moment must be considered in connection with
any commitments for the immediate future. Mr. Parker said
that he realized this; that his company had not made new
investments in Japan for sometime back; and that he
supposed that, more and more, the Japanese would see to
it that, where their own people could compete with
foreigners doing business in Japan, little if any profit
woula

=== Page 147 === (confidence 0.95)
- 4 -
would be permitted to acorue to the latter. I suggested
that this part of the conversation be regarded as strictly
entre nous. Mr. Parker said: "Of course". Mr. Parker said
he appreciated all the trouble which we had taken in con-
nection with this matter. I said that it was not trouble;
that it was our business; that what concerns the rights
and interests of American business concerns us; and that
we appreciate the disposition to consult us and "play the
game with" us which his company has shown. I wished him
an enjoyable and successful trip. And the conversation
there ended.

Related Developments and Problems.
Mr. Teagle called Mr. Mackay on the telephone from
New York. Mr. Teagle stated that he had just received
from his company's representative in London a cable to
tho effect that the London director of the Royal Dutch
Shell interests reoently had a further disoussion with
the British Foreign Offioe; that it is believed that the
Foreign Office will sometime during this week take action
with regard to the oil situation in Japan and Manchuria;
that the conoerned British and Dutch oil interests are
strongly of the opinion that the Japanese Government will
take no action of consequence if the oil companies fail
to file before November 15 their statements of intention
in regard to the storage provisions of the Petroleum
D
Industry Law; and that the representatives of the oil
interests in London are now of the opinion, possibly as a
result of a suggestion from the Foreign Office, that the
Japanese Government should not be informed that ranking
F/G
officials

=== Page 159 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
officials of the oil companies are now en route to China.
Mr. Teagle requested an expression of opinion with regard
to whether the American oil interests should refrain from
informing the Japanese Government that their representa-
tive is about to sail for Yokohama.
Mr. Mackay replied to the effect that in his opinion
the Department would not care to express an opinion an
suoh a matter; that the companies should decide for them-
selves with regard to tactics or strategies to be em-
ployed; that the reasons for withholding from the Japa-
nese Government information with regard to the impending
arrival in Japan of officials of the interested oil
companies are by no means clear; but that if practicable
it would seem highly desirable that the American, British
and Dutoh interests present a united front to the Japa-
nese Government.
Mr. Teagle replied that he likewise failed to under-
stand the reasoning behind the existing attitude in
London; that he hoped, however, to obtain a clarifioation
by means of a telephone conversation which he would have
about noon today with his representative in London; and
that he might, depending upon the information so received,
telephone the Department this afternoon.
Mr. Teagle expressed his appreciation for all the
Department is doing on behalf of the American oil inter-
ests and stated that he would call at the Department to-
morrow, November l4, for a further disoussion of the situation.

November 13th, 1934.
MOMORANDUM IN REGARD TO JAPAN.
Information has been received by us today from our
and Shell representatives in London that efter discussion with british
Foreign Office by Mr. Agnew, the latter still feel that the British
Foreign Office will take the initiative, paving in this way for full
discussion of the Petroleum Law by the fore in companies with the
Japanese Government and that the latter will definitely modify its
views as long as no woakening is shown by the foreign companies. Mr.
Agnew of Shell believes action will be taken by the Foreign Office
this week and that it is the understanding that the British Ambassador
at Tokyo will be asked to support the Shell's interests in this at-
titude. Friends ir. London heve no apprehension as a result of tempor-
arily disregarding the law, pending the ultimate discussion. Suggest-
ions are made by representatives of both companies in London that it
would be a mistake to approach the Japanese Government now for postpone-
ment of presentation of the information required by them and tley
strongly advocate that the foreign vil companies should ignore the de-
mand for completion of the required returns by November 15th. They
further feel that it would be a mistake to open up direct discussion
with the Japanese Government at a time when diplomatic negotiations are
in progress and they feel that the Japanese Government will definitely
modify its views in respect to the provisions of the new law if no
weakening is shown by the foreign companies.
Assurances are given that diplomatic pressure by the
British Foreign Office will be exerted this week in an effort to pave
the way for disoussions between the foreign oil companies and the

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
Japanese authorities in respect to modifications in the operation of
the law.
In consideration of these views and as we understend it will
not conflict with any State Department plans, Standard-Vacuum Oil
Company proposes to instruct its representative in Japan to take no
action on or before November 15th in resrect to completion of the form
as demanded by the Japanese Governent. Inasmuch as sinilar instructions
have been telegraphed by Shell, London, to their representative, both
interests will presert a united front at Tokyo.
The substance of the two messages from London - dated
November 12th and November 13th - was telephoned today by I'r. Teagle
to Mr. McKay of the State Department.

dated October 19, 1934, and to previous correspondence in
regard to the problems of the American and other foreign
oil interests in Japan and to report that no important
developments have taken place during the past two weeks
except the receipt of a reply from the Foreign Office
to the Embassy's oral representations of September
FLLED
25, 1934, made in compliance with the Department's
76
telegraphic instruction No. 162, September 21, 8 p.m.
The
F1

=== Page 170 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
The reply from the Foreign Office was in the form
of an informal memorandum, dated Öctober 31, 1934, of
which identic copies (except for the opening sentence)
were sent to the American and British Embassies and the
Netherlands Legation. A translation of the memorandum
has been made and checked 'ith the British and Netherlands
translations, and therefore is believed to be accurate.
The gist of the memorandum was contained in y telegram
No. 241, November 1, 6 p.m., and the ful1 text is trans-
1/ mitted herewith. The memorandum, it will be observed,
gives no assurances that the foreign oil companies
will be able to continue their present business indefi-
nitely, but rather implies that should the present rate
of annual increase of denand decline, the allotments of
the foreign firms will be decreased; it states definitely
that it is Japan's policy in general to refine iported
crude oil rather than to import the refined products, and
that therefore the increase in demand will first be al-
lotted to such refining plants as huve idle equipment
and the remainder will be allotted to other refiners and
importers; it vaguely invites the importers of gasoline
to import crude oil and refine it in Jipan and states
that permission to conduct such refining will be more
easily obtained if more than half of the capital of the
company is Japanese; it disclaims any intention on the
part of the Government to take over the companies' stocks
of oil except in case of urgent necessity and then only at
current prices; and it further disclaims any intention of
arbitrarily regulating the market prices of oils, although
the

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
the right to regulate prices is reserved for use when con-
sidered necessary for the public welfare or for the in-
terests of industry. No mention is made in the memorandum
of the burdensome stock-holding recuirements, which were
the principal subject of our representations to the J pa-
nese Government.
The Foreign Office memorandum is considered as most
unsatisfactory by the American and british oil interests
in Japan. Not only does it not agree to any concessions
in regard to stock-holding requirements, but its assurances
as to the future business of the foreign oil companies are
too vague and too greatly hedged with conditions to be ac-
ceptable. Tne only concession made is the somewhat dubious
one of informing the oil companies that they will be better
off if they agree to refine in Japan, instead of importing
the refined products, especially if they give Japanese capital
the controlling interest in the refineries. It is not be-
lieved that either the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company or the
Rising Sun Petroleum Company is as yet ready to turn its
business over to Japanese interests in the manner indicated.
From the tone of the memorandum it would appear that
the Japanese are not inclined to recede from their stand
in regard to the control of the oil situation in Japan. The
TOKYO ASAHI, however, on November 1, 1934, published an
article indicating that the Government is considering some
measures which will impose less burdensome restrictions on
the foreign firms. The article states that the Japanese
Government recognized the losses which would be caused to
the oil companies as a result of the stock-holding
requirements

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.97)
-4-
requirements of the Petroleum Industry Law, but could not
itself bear the cost of storage of oil. It is now of the
opinion that the position of the oil firms should be re-
considered, and may take some administrative measures to
relax the strain on the foreign companies, such as extending
the period in which the storage requirements have to be met
or revising the import quotas, according to the ASAHI.
No further steps have been taken by the foreign oil
companies in the matter of complying with the stock-holding
demands of the Petroleum Industry Law. They are now marking
time in the hope that some amelioration may be obtainable.
Respectfully yours,
o<
Joseph C. Grew.

(SEAL OF FOREIGN OFFICE)
Memorandum.
The Imperial Japanese Government has carefully
deliberated upon the question of the oil industry, con-
cerning which the American Chargé d'Affairs ad interim
on September 25, 1934, called on and conversed with the
Minister for Foreign Affairs. The object of the Petro-
leum Industry Law and the policy of the Imperial Japa-
nese Government in the application of the same are as
stated below and there is no intention thereby to de-
prive those engaged in the oil industry of security in
their operations. The Imperial Japanese Government
accordingly hopes that the American oil companies con-
cerned may be so informed and that they may be urged
to cooperate with the Japanese oil industry in con-
formity with the provisions of the Petroleum Industry
Law.
(1) The requirement that Government sanction must
be obtained annually for the operating plans of an oil
refining business or of an oil importing business is
based on the object of regulating the import, produc-
tion and sale of oil, together with that of guiding
and supervising the achievement of smooth operations in
the industry; there is no intention of ignoring the
interests of those engaged in the oil industry or of
impairing the continuous character of the industry.
Accordingly, in the enforcement of the Petroleum
Industry Law, so long as the Japanese oil demands con-
tinue their present tendency to increase year after
year

=== Page 174 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
year, no restriction below the limits of their business
prior to the enforcement of the Law will be imposed upon
the operations of those engaged in the oil industry.
(2) In future the distribution among those
engaged in the industry of an increase in the demand
for oil is to be determined at the time of annual
sanction of operating plans, regard being paid to the
trend of domestic supply and demand, circumstances in
the oil refining industry, the situation in the oil
importing business, and various other conditions,
although this cannot be definitely determined in
advance. But as Japan generally speaking stands in
the position of desiring the refining of i..ported crude
oil rather than the importation of the refined product,
and in view of the existing situation of idle refining
plants, it is intended, first of all, to allot to them
such amounts of the increase in demand as are suitable
for rational operation, and to allot fairly the remainder
to all refiners and importers.
(3) The granting or denying of permission for
oil refining operations will be decided in actual cases
by deliberations of the Petroleum Committee, regard
being paid to the trend of domestic supply and demand,
the circumstances of those engaged in refining, and
various other conditions, although it will be difficult
to announce the granting or denying of permission un-
less the above committee has received for consideration
concrete operating plans. But those companies engaged
in the importation of gasoline may, within the limits of
the

=== Page 175 === (confidence 1.00)
-3-
the volume of imports allotted to them in accordance
with the two preceding paragraphs, import, in place
of refined products, crude oil, and in the cases of
those undertaking such domestic refining operations,
except in special circumstars, sanction may be ob-
tained comparatively easily; particularly, those enter-
prises of more than half Japanese capital will find it
conducive to the smooth carrying out of operations,
and a result will be brought about of sanction being
granted all the more easily.
(4) Although the Government may, according to
the stipulations of Article 6 of the Petroleum In-
dustry Law, in cases where the urgent public interest
demands it, purchase oil owned by those engaged in the
oil industry, nevertheless the market price will be made
the standard according to the above-mentioned Article
and Article 8 of the Enforcing Act, and there will be no
unfair pressure upon those engaged in the industry.
Secondly, the companies engaged in the oil industry may
in principle sell oil freely within the limits of opera-
ting plans approved by the Government, and the Government
will not interfere arbitrarily with the selling-price
of oil. However, the Government in order to protect
the public interest and the interests of industry in
general may, when there is a recognized necessity in
accordance with Article 7 of the Petroleum Industry Lew,
order changes in the selling price, but there is no in-
tention of disturbing industrial equilibrium or of im-
pairing the interests of those engaged in the industry.
FAS.
October 31, 1934.

law for the purehase or the equipment of existing

lieensed by the government may manufacture or

the government. Retail sales shall be handled by

faot law is so general that it is impossible to

oil companies.

government of the equipment of those in the
petroleum business who are forced out of business
by the monopoly law at a valuation to be fixed by
an appraisal commission the organization of which
is to be determined by ordinance.
"The date of enforcement of these laws, and
regulations covering exclusive agents and other
matters are to be fixed by the Minister of Finance."

The British Ambassador asked me Whether I felt that
we were satisfied with the cooperation of the British with
respeot to the oil probleme in Japan and in "Manchukuo."
I recited briefly our policy of declared willingness to co-
894.6363/1
operate with the British and reminded him that they had taken
the first step with us, to which we had replied most sympa-
thetioally; that we had made certain representations in
Tokyo and that it was, in our opinion, up to the oil companies
themselves to make their independent representations, as they
29

sentatives to Tokyo; so far as the oil situation in Japan
was concerned, it seemed to me that it was largely a busi-
ness proposition and that, unless the oil companies took a
decided position themselves, it was somewhat difficult for
this government to act.
The Ambassador gave the impression that the Foreign
Office was slightly piqued at our attitude in trying to throw
the greater responsibility for action upon the British Govern-
ment, to which I replied that we naturally assumed they desired
to take it, inasmuch as they had the larger interests and that

Mr. Hornbeck called Mr. Coe on long-distance tele-
phone. Mr. Hornbeck said that he had some information
to impart and a question to ask. He then gave Mr. Coe
the substance of Peiping's telegram 535, November 23,
3 p.m. Mr. Coe said that he had seen substantially that
information in the press. Mr. Hornbeck then inquired
what was Mr. Coe's understanding with regard to the sub-
jects which the representatives of the oil companies now
en route to Japan would discuss with the Japanese authori-
ties: was it to be the Japanese situation and the
Manchuria situation or only one of the two. Mr. Coe
replied that he understood that it was to be the Japanese
situation only; that there had seemingly been some dif-
FTLFD
ference of opinion with regard to what was advisable in
that connection; and that possibly as the situation unfolded
they would discuss both. Mr. Hornbeck inquired whether
Mr. Coe knew what was the view of the British oil interests.
F
Mr. Coe
G

=== Page 189 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Mr. Coe said that he thought that they were inclined to
link the two subjeots but that he was not certain. Mr.
Hornbeck requested that, if Mr. Coe obtained anything more
definite on that point, he let us know. Mr. Coe said that
he would do so.
Mr. Hornbeck then inquired whether Mr. Coe had any-
thing new with regard to these matters. Mr. Coe said that
he had nothing. Mr. Hornbeck inquired whether there was
anything new with regard to London. Mr. Coe replied that
he understood that the British interests there had again
been assured by the Foreign Office that the Foreign Office
would take further action and that it would probably be
early next week. Mr. Hornbeck inquired whether Mr. Coe
knew anything about the nature of the action thus contem-
plated. Mr. Coe said that he did not and apparently the
British interests did not.
Mr. Coe then said that he was just preparing something
to send to us, the latest communications which he had
received. Mr. Hornbeok inquired whether Mr. Coe knew
whether his representative, Goold, in Japan, had informed
the American Ambassador of the expedition of representatives
of the interested companies to Japan. Mr. Coe said that
11
11

Reference, my memorandun of conversation with Lr.
1131
Wiggin on the same subject on November 17.
I called Mr. Wiggin on the 'phone this morning and said
32
to him, referring back to our conversation of the l7th, that
I would be glad to give him, for the British Embassy's infor-
mation, an account of certain points which might be interesting
and helpful to the Embassy. Mr. Wiggin came to my office. I
said to Mr. Wiggin that the British and the American Govern-
ments have each taken certain steps, through their respective
Ambassadors in Tokyo, with regard to these oil questions and
that, so far as we know, there has been a complete exchange
of information with regard to each such step taken. I told
Mr. Wiggin of the meeting of the representatives of the
oil companies concerned, in London; and I said that we had
reason to believe that the British interests have been in
close touch with the British Foreign Office and have kept
the Foreign Office informed. I said that we had made it
F
known
See memnandun 7 Nn. 17,1934
in same subjict

=== Page 193 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
known to the Foreign Office through our Embassy in London
many weeks ago that, in reply to an approach made by the
Foreign Office to the Embassy, on the subject of cooperative
action, we would be prepared to "give sympathetic considera-
tion" to any suggestions which the British Foreign Office
might care to make. I said that we had since then had no
word from the British Foreign Office on this subject; but
that on November 12 we had been informed by the American
interests that they had been informed by the British interests
that there was reason to believe that the British Foreign
Office would make some move "before the end of the week"
(November 17). I said that we had had no further word in
the matter. I concluded with the statement that I was
giving this information simply for the purpose of orienting
the Embassy and not with any intent of suggesting to the
Embassy that it report further to the Foreign Office on the
subject: but that the Embassy might perhaps tell the Foreign
Office that we had given them this account of the matter
and had said that we believed that the Foreign Office, by
virtue of the fact that the oil interests have been conferring
in London, was probably more completely informed of the views
of the interests, especially the British interests, and the
plans of the interests, arrived at at London, than are we.
I said that I believed that the attitude of the Foreign
orfice

=== Page 194 === (confidence 0.96)
- 3 -
Office with regard to the whole matter is identical with
ours and that we are assuming that the Foreign Office will
make suggestions when it considers doing so opportune and
convenient.
Mr. Wiggin thanked me for imparting this information.
He said that the Embassy had reported on the conversation
held on Saturday and had asked the Foreign Office for
clearer instruction.
The conversation there ended.

PERSONAL AND CONFI ENTIAL FOR HORNBECK FROM DOOMAN.
NO DISTRIBUTION.
"Millard and I called this morning on Orde at
his invitation to discuss the points raised in the Depart-
67
ment's 347,/August 31, noon. 1 stated to Orde that I
had no authority to speak officially, but that I thought
it mint be helpful if we talked informally.
Orde remarked that the British oil industry is
unified and that therefore the British are not faced
7
with tho problem which he understood exists in the
United States, of securing concerted action within the
industry; he stated that the British oil concerns are
already in a position to take effective measures to meot
the situation, although they would naturally first asur
themselvos that such measures as they had under conten
plation would not be injurious to British policy and
national
F
K

=== Page 224 === (confidence 0.99)
REP
2-#603, From London, Nov. 27, 7 p. m.
national interests. I said that the circumstances are
different in the United States, but that it was my
understanding that the American petroleum industry is
well organized.
After further conversation, Orde stated that the
British Government would regard with favor concerted
action between the British and American oil concerns
on the following conditions:
First, any measure that may be agreed upon should
be water-tight or at any rate effective.
Second, no formal action shall be required of the
British Government.
Third, the situations in Japan and in Manchuria
are to be dealt with as two parts of the same problem
(in other words, any measure to be taken shall be
applied to both areas or not at all).
Orde, who is by temperament extremely cautious,
nevertneless showed quite plainly that he is eager to
have the British and American oil concerns place them-
selves in a strong position vis a vis the Japanese. He
seemed doubtful of the wisdom of making in the present
circumstances a further official demarche at Tokio. Ho
said that the British Government and oil interests have
not

=== Page 225 === (confidence 0.99)
REP
3-#603, From London, Nov. 27, 7 p.m.
not conclusively decided that any measure of the fore-
going character should be applied as soon as agreement
thereon can be reached, but that they believe that it would
be advantageous to give the oil interests freedom to take
action. He thought that if any effective private measure
can be devised by the oil interests of the three countries
concerned, a real and effective basis would be laid for
further official representations."
BINGHAM

Dear Dr. Hornbeck:
000
In our conversation of Novamber 27th with
Mr. Mackay, we promised we would send to the Department, a
copy of a recent letter we wrote Yokohama, in which the high-
lights of the French and Japanese Petroleum Laws were compared.
We have pleasure in attaching this copy, although we are not
sending a copy of Ir. Henry's letter to us, W ich is simply an
amplification of our remarks.
It will be observed we stated we had filed
with you a short brief on the Japanese Law and we believe we
are correct in saying this, as Ir. Teagle, in a letter from
Washington under date of September 20th, stated he had an ap-
pointment with you on September 2lst, at which time he would
leave the brief in question at your office. Consequently we
are not sending a copy of this.
We expect to communicate with you within the
next day or so in respect to cables we will dispatch to friends
in London and to our Yokohama Office.
I wish to express my appreciation for the time
the Department gave me yesterday and the interesting conversa-
tion I had with Mr. Mackay, which resulted in the clarification
of several points which had been obscure.
Sincerely yours,
Lecdry F. Gor

Mr. Mackay called Mr. Coe by long distance telephone

plied that the letter under reference was at that moment
before him and that he was on the point of telephoning the
Department in regard thereto. Mr. Coe stated that he de-
sired to discuss the situation with the Department as soon
as praoticable and asked whether an appointment for 10:30
a.m., Tuesday, November 27, 1934, would be convenient.
Mr. Maokay replied that he would, of course, be pleased to
receive Mr. Coe at 10:30 or at any other time which suited

Mr. Coe's convenience. Mr. Coe stated that he wished to
make the appointment fairly late in the morning in order
FILED

the Department, last minute information from his New York
office.

of his understanding that the American Embassy at Tokyo is
fully informed in regard to the fact that Mr. Parker and
certain other senior officers of the principally inter-
ested oil companies are now en route to Japan. Mr. Mackay
stated that inasmuoh as Mr. Coe was not positive that such

what vaguely phrased; to which telegram the Embassy had

Mr. Mackay suggested that as, with a view to maintaining

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

December 5, 1934.
MSTT
Despatch No. 1060 of November 16, 1934,
from Tokyo:
The Japanese Department of Commerce
and Industry refused to grant tre foreign
oil companies a postponement of time by
which the supplemental plans for importa-
tion and storage of oil had to be submitted.
The British and Dutch have urged tleir
governments that steps be taken to control
the export of crude oil to Japan if the
Japanese persist in comnelling the foreign
oil companies to store a nart of treir
war-time reserves of oil for them. The British
Government has instructed the British Embassy
to support the British oil company in its
refusal to comply with the demand.
It appears that as a result of
diplomatic protests and adverse comments
abroad, there is some tendency on the part
of the authorities to mitigate the severity
of the oil storage requirements.
There is little doubt that the stock
holding provision of the law is a military
measure. This being the case, it is
possible that a new line of attack on the
law might be feasible, by claiming that
forced

=== Page 279 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
forced storing of oil by American firms
conflicts with the Treaty of Commerce
which provides that Americans:
"Shall --- be exempt from ---
all ---military exactions or contri-
butions".

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,

Sir:
I have the honor to refer to my despatch No. 1042
12L
dated November 2, 1934, and to previous correspondence,
written and telegraphic, in regard to the difficulties of
the American and other foreign oil interests in Japan under
the new Petroleum Industry Law, and to submit the following
report of developments during the past two weeks.
Representations.
1105
In my telegram No. 241,'November 1, 6 p.m., in my des-
1126
patch No. 1042, dated November 2, 1934, and in the transla-
tion of the Foreign Office Memorandum of October 31, 1934,
which

=== Page 281 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
which was transnitted with the despatch referred to above,
it was stated that permission to refine petroleum in Japan
would be more easily granted if "more than half" of the
capital of the refining company vere Japanese. The phrase
translated as "more than alf" in thy Japancse text was
"Hangaku ijo", of which th itcral translation is "half
a ount above", and consecuently this Enbassy, thc British
Enbassy and the Nethorlean s Le ation all translatcd the
expression as "more than half". In discussing the question,
however, the Japanese newspepers referred to "50-50" capi-
talization of refining firms, an consecuently thc Embassy
asked the Foreign Office for elucidation of the phrase.
The Foreign Of ice itself vas rot clear on thr point and
asked the Ministry of Conterce und Industry (which apparently
drafted the  e crandum) for an explanation. According to
the explanation given, the phrase "hangaku ijo" was used
in the memorandum in a legal sense which it sometimes has,
in which it means "at least half" or "not less than half".
Consequently the Embassy sent its telegram No. 51, Novem-
ber 13, 4 p.m., recuesting that the neceszary changes be
made in the telegram and t anslation sent to the Department
on the subject. The Embassy is informed that the same
change was made by the British Embassy.
The meaning of thc phrase has some consicerable import-
ance, as, while thc foreign oil companies operating in
Japan would not be likely, according to the statements of
their representatives in Japan, to consicer refining in
Japan if the control of the local refining concerns which

oils to Japan if the Japanese  ersist in compelling the

amelioration of the situation unless the foreign oil companies
and the American, British and Netherlands Governments pre-

It appears that, as a result of the diplomatic representa-
tions which have been made, of the adverse comment abroad
which has be en caused by the publicity given the uestions
of the oil monopoly in Manchuria and the oil control in Japan,
and of the gra ual realization on the part of the Japanese
authorities of the unworkability of their scheme in its pre-
sent form, ther is now some tendency on the part of the au-
thorities to mitigate the severity of the stock-holding re-
quirements. This tondency is illustrated by three conversa-
tions which members of the staff of the Embassy have had
3/4/5/
with Japanese officials, memoranda of hich are enclosed. The
first was a conversation which Mr. Dickover had with Vice
Admiral Hasegawa, Vice Minister of the Navy, on November 5,
1934, and the other two were conversations which Mr. Neville
had with Mr. Kurusu, Chief of the Bureau of Commercial Af-
fairs of the Foreign Office, on November 13 and November 14,
1934.
The only fruit borne as yet of this tendency to mitigate
the severity of the stock-holding provisions of the law con-
sists of a probable concession as regards what will constitute
the six-months' stock required by th: regulations. Formerly
the requirements were that the stock must be held in tanks of
a stipulated minimum size located in places designated by the
Government.

=== Page 285 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
Government. These conditions practically excluded from
the six months' stock the oil firms' working stocks, which
normally were largely in storage tanks at the sea-ports,
in tank-cars, in tank lighters, in small distribution
tanks at inland points, in service st tion t nks, in c ns,
etc. Under date of November 14, 1934, horever, Mr. Kurusu,
the Chief of the Commercial Bureau of the Foreign Office,
transmitted to this Embasey and to the British Embassy an
explanation in English of the stock-holding regulations,
in which it is statec that, subject to the permission of
the Minister of Com erce and Industry, the cil may be held
The explanation further st: tes that only three months!
stock must be held in storage prior to October 1, 1935,
and implies that the six months' stock may be gradually built
up, over a period of twelve months, after October 1, l935.
Accorcing to the representatives of the foreign oil companies,
however, this concession will have little practical effect,
as technical difficulties will prevent the oil con panies from
including the largest part of thcir working stocks in the
storage stocks. It will, however, make a difference of from
two to three weeks' stocks. Despite this concession, there-
fore, the great bulk of the six months' storage stocks would
still have to be imported and helc as idle stocks.
A copy of the letter from Mr. Kurusu, with its enclosures,
6/
is enclosed for the information of the Department.

refusing to listen to any explanations or comprorise plans
which the authorities might have to offer. The various
dangers referred to above they decided to avoid by stating
explicitly that they had no authority to comnit their com-
panies, as the power of decision la in their hea offices,
and that, moreover, as their Governments were inter sted in
the question and were discussing it with the Japanese Govern-
ment, anything said would be conveyed to their respective
Embassies. The conference wil? prob bly he held in the
early part of next week.
STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL.
There is little doubt that the stock-holding provision
of the Petroleum Industry Law is largely a military measure,
designed

=== Page 287 === (confidence 0.98)
-8 -
designed to build up reserves of oil in Japan for use
in case of war. It may be difficult to ad uce evidence
that any official pronouncement was ever made to this
effect, but corsiderable unofficial evicence can be found
7/
to support this cont ntion. Thus, there is enclosed here-
with an excerpt from a specch maoe by Mr. K. dashimoto,
the President of th Mipoon Oil Co  any, on October 27,
1934, before the stockholders of the Nippon Oil Company,
in which the statement is definitely made that the stock-
holding provision is a measure of national defense. Viewed
in this light, it is respectfully sug  ted that a new line
of attack on the stock-hol ing provisions might be acopted,
by claiming that forced storing of oil by Anerican firms to
provide a war-time reserve for Japan conflicts with the
last paragraph of rticle 1 of the Treaty of Commerce and
Navigation of 19ll between the United States and Japan,
which reads as follows:
"They shall, however, be exempt in the
territories of the othor ro compulsory
military service either on land or sea,
in the regular forces, or in the national
guard, or in the milita; from all contri-
butions imposed in lieu of personal service,
and from all forced loans or military exac-
tions or contributions."
If the Department considers that there is any possibility
that this line of action could be adopted, the Embassy will
gather and transmit to the Department all possible evidence
indicating

=== Page 288 === (confidence 0.96)
- 9 -
indcating that the stock-holding recuirement is a "military
exaction".
Respectfully yours,
2.
Joseph C. Grew

MEMORANDUM
November 6, 1934.
Conversation:
Mr. Dickover and Vice Admiral Hasegawa,
Vice Minister of the Navy.
During the course of the conversation at ainner last
evening, I told Admiral Hasegawa that the oil question had
not, as far as I knew, been brought up purposely at this
time, when the naval conversations vere taking place, and
that it was simoly a coincidence, caused by the Ministry of
Commerce and Inaustry insisting at the moment on the storage
of oil by the foreign firms. Admiral Hasegawa said that he
understood that. I asked Ad iral Hasegawa what he thought
of the Japanese oil control scheme. He saia that he aic not
think that it was very good- that it was not workable.
I said that the plan of forcing the oil companies to
store six months' supply of oil was comnercially impossible;
that for the Japanese oil companies it might be a patriotic
gesture for which they would sacrifice something, but that
for the foreign oil companies, who naturally were looking
to profits, the cheme was not possible, as they could not
make enough profit in Japan under the quota system to pay
interest on the increased investment.
The Admiral said that that was his opinion also; that
the scheme was commercially impossible; and that he thought
that the law would have to be altered in favor of the foreign
oil companies.

Petroleum Industry Law.

=== Page 298 === (confidence 0.99)
Explanations on Legal Obligation to Hold Qil.
By virtue of Art icle 5 of the Oil Law, Article 6 of
the Imperial Ordinance relating to the Application of the
Oil Law (hereinafter referred to as "the Ordinance") and
Article 16 of the Regulations relating to the Application
of the Oil Law (hereinafter referred to as "the Regulations"),
any person who imports oil is under obligation to hold this
substance to the extent of not less than one-half of the
quantity imported by him in the preceding year. The oil
to be so held must be kept, as a matter of principle,
in tanks of a prescribed capacity, under Article 7 of the
Ordinance and Article 19 of the Regulations; but, if the
permission of the Minister of Commerce and Industry is
obtained in accordance with the proviso of Paragraph 1,
Article 7 of the Ordinance and under Article 20 of the
Regulations, the said oil may be held in tanks of less
than the prescribed capacity and also may be held by
methods other than that of tankage. It is understood
thatthe Department of Commerce and Industry, with a view
to alleviating the difficulties to which the merchants
concerned may be put, will grant the above-mentioned
permission in so far as the methods of holding oil for
which they may apply are adequate and practicable for the
purpose of the Law.
The obligation to hold oil comes into force on April
1st, 1935. For the priod from April 1st to September 30th,
1935, the quantity of oil to be held must not be less than
one-half of the quantity imported during the six months
from

=== Page 299 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
from July 1st to December 31st, 1934- that is to say,
no more than three months' imports. In the light of
Article 16 of the Regulations, it may seem that the quan-
tity of oil to be held during the period from April 1st
to September 30th, 1935, must not be less than one-half
of the quantity cleared through the Customs between January
1st and December 3lst, 1934, but this is not the case. The
explanation is that, as the Oil Law did not come into force
until July 1st, 1934, no account is taken of the quantity
imported prior to that date.
If, in the ordinary course of business, oil importers
hold in stock a quantity of oil corresponding approximately
to two or three months' imports, as is generally considered
to be the case, it ust not be particularly difficult for
them to fulfil their obligation of holding oil, since the
quantity to be held for the period from April 1st to Sep-
tember 30th, 1935, is not much in excess of the quantity
usually held in stock. Moreover, in storing oil, they
need not necessarily adopt the tanks specified in Article
19 of the Regulations as mentioned above. It is true that
from Uctober 1st, 1935, onwards, the quantity of oil to
be retained on hand will increase to a figure corresponding
to six months' imports, in the course of twelve months,
but even then that part of this increase which is due to the
Oil Law and is in excess of the ordinary stock is no more than
three or four months' imports.
The merchants concerned will be well-advised to form
their concrete business plans as early as possible and make
the

=== Page 300 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
the necessary application to the Department of Commerce
and Industry, and they have also to apply to that Depart-
ment for permission to use their existing equipment for
holding oil where such equipment is of a kind available for
the purpose. The Department will naturally consider only
cases in which applications are made, and if this necessary
procedure is neglected, they will have no means of exercising
favourable consideration.

Encosure No
to despatch
No 1060 of 0. 1b,144
from the

Excerpt for Speech made by r. K. Hashimoto,
Presicent of th: Nippon Oil Company, before
te Stockholders of the Nippon Oil Company,
October 27, 1934.
Referring to the stock-holding uty provi ed for
in the Petroleum Industry Law, it means a very heavy
burden on the oil importers generally, inasmuch as it makes
it necessary for them to erect storage-tanks, and the possi-
ble evaporation los: on their stocks (the Law requires the
holding of stocks covering six months' recuirements), in-
terest on the money tied up, etc. amount to a considerable
sum. From our business point of view, it is decidedly un-
necessary for us to hold so large a quantity of oil. It is
quite obvious that the stock-holding reguirement is for
the sake of national defence. The Government thercfore
ought to pay the expenses for this duty. Al! the oil com-
panies presented a joint appeal to the Government in this
connection. Ve are told that the G vernment is consider-
ing it.

Standard-Vacuum Oil Company,
YOKOHAMA.
O11 Monopoly - Manchoukuo.
Dear Sirs:-
We are forwarding herewith copy of Dairen Consulate
letter of October 24th to our Moukden office as we feel it will
be of some interest to you and our Moukden letter does not indi-
cate whether or not a copy has been sent to you.
The possibility of a control law similar to the
Japanese law has been considered, but we have never felt it
desirable to suggest such a compromise owing to some of the
undesirable fea tures of the Japanese law and our uncertainty
as to your acceptance of the Japanese law as it now stands.
Recent reports from Manchuria indicate that some such comprom-
ise will present the only alterna tive to evacua ting the terri-
tory entirely. Naturally, any suggestion of a control law
with quota arrangements for the established companies will
depend entirely on the present sta tus of your negotiations
with the Japanese Government, and we will appreciate your
opinion as to the advi sability of such a proposal and the
probability of its success, if made.
As kerosene sales in the Manchurian territory during
the years 1932 and 1933 were far below normal, and the market
for gasoline is rapidly expanding, we would wish to have any
quota arrangement based on percentage of total trede re ther
than fixed quantities. The price control feature will be
undesirable, but there is a fair probability that prices will
be maintained at profitable levels in Manchuria as the refinery
at Dairen presumably will not be operating under a preferential
tariff.
Today's newspaper reports the resignation of General
Hishikari in Manchuria and we are interested in knowing whether
or not this will change the outlook for the oil companies in
Manchuria. As you know,Mr.Hashimoto, head of the Manchoukuo
Oil Monopoly Bureau, is a brother-in-law of General Hishikari
and our Moukden office have several times ascribed to this
connection the success of the officials in pressing the monopoly
idea. Your opinion on the effect of this change will be much
appreciated.
Yours very truly,
STANDA RD-VACUUM OIL COMPANY
(Signed) F J Twogood

The receipt is acknowledged of your le tters of

for Manchuria. A copy of your le tter No.llo6 to your

agont have been forwarded by airmail to the Embassy at

For your information I quote below pertinent extracts from
my despatch transmitting your Shanghai letter No.llo6 to
the Embassy:
"I am inclined to doubt the Mukden Manager's de-
duction that the 'officials at Hsinching are so far
working on pure bluff' but I do believe that they are
not yet sure enough of their ground to be heedless of
protests and proposals for modifice tion. I feel that
it is imperative thet the foreign oil companies formu-
late as soon as possible mutually agreeable counter
proposals and not, as in the past, be content only with
protesting directiy and through their diplometic re-
presentatives on the be sis of violetion of the 'open door'
and equal opportunity. That some form of control over
the oil business in Manchuria will be established in the
near future seems to me e foregone conclusion, and the
amount of business which the foreign oil comp&nies will
be able to re tain will depend largely on the unity, de-
termination, and wisdom with which theymeet the situation.
They may look to their governments for supnort but they
must take the initiative in formulating a plen of defense.
"It is possible thet insistence on retaining the
privilege of marketing refined oil in Manchuria, accom-
panied by agreement to submit to a control and quota system
would,when backed by diplomatic protests, be effective,
In the matter of quotes, the foreign oil companies col-
lectively would be justified on the basis of present
sales, in demanding seventy-five to eighty per cent of
the market. Allowance must be made however for the pro-
ducts of the Manchuria Oil Company's refinery at Dairen
which,regardless of other developments, are going to be
sold in Manchuria. It now appears that this refinery

=== Page 320 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
roduce 7t million gallons of refined oil a year.
represents 20 per cent of the l933 imports and about
y be the imports for l934. Hence a concession by the
foreign oil ompanies to submit to reasonable control and
to restrict their sales to roughly 65 per cent of the mar-
ket, while a forfeiture of 'open door' rights and un-
warranted on the basis of their share of the market over
a period of years, might form the basis of reaching an
agreement, whereas an uncompromising demand for the con-
tinuation of free competi tive marketing would, I fear, be
unavailaing."
I should appreciate receiving your comments on
these observations I have made to the Embassy. It is of
course impossible to calculate with any degree of certainty
the extent to which the Hsinching authorities are willing
to compromise, assuming they are willing to compromise at
all. This can only be determined through negotiations.
I very much approve of your idea for a meeting at the very
earliest moment of representatives of the foreign oil
companies concerned with representatives of the Americen,
British, and Dutch embassies and I believe thet this
meeting should be in Japan. Having decided upon some
common policy, embassy and oil representa tives should
arrange a conference with Japanese Foreign Office to
protest the monopoly and, as it will probebly be necessary,
propose some alternative plan. I believe thet every
offort should be made to avoid a conference with the
Hsinching authorities until the case is laid fully and
forcibly before the Japanese Foreign Office. Unless some
degree of support can be obtained from the Japanese
Government, I do not think conference with the Hsinching
authorities would avail anything. Time is an importent
element because once the oil monopoly law and regulations
are published it may be well nigh i possible to obta in
alteration or modifica tion.
Yours truly,
John Carter Vincent

Dear Dr. Hornbeck:
In reply to a cable we sent London on
Nov. 28th, in which we again inguired regarding further representa-
9
tions to be made by the Foreign Office in respect to the Petroleum
3
Control Law in Japan, the Hew York Office of Shell interests has
11
notified us that it received Nov. 3oth, a cable which in part read:
"Have every reason to believe all parties concerned are now
closely in touch and thet full exchange of views will take place.
3
Inasmuch as we have acquiesced in the position
established abroad tnat joint diplomatic representations by Govern-
ments should take place prior to discussions between American and
British oil representatives and Japanese officials, we are naturally
hopeful that these representations be made prior to the arrival in
Japan of Messrs. Godber, Walden and Parker.
Since our last conversation with you, we wish to
confirm the fact that Mr. Parker has proceeded to Shanghai, that
Mr. Walden was due to arrive there on Dec. 3rd, and that they both
will await Mr. Godber at that point. The latter should be in
Shanghai Dec. 17th, and it is assumed that these three gentlemen
will be in Japan sometime between Dec. 20th and 25th.
We have received an abstract of an address, before
his stockholders, of Ir. Hashimoto, President of the Nippon Oil
Company and also President of the Manchu Oil Company. It is particu-
larly interesting to note that he openly states his views upon the
stockholding regulations of the Control Law as follows:-
"Referring to the stockholding duty provided for in the
Petroleum Industry Law, it means a very heavy burden on the oil
importers generally, inasmuch as it makes it necessary for them
to erect storage tanks, and the possible evaporation loss on
their stocks (the Law requires the holding of stocks covering
six months' requirements), interest on the money tied up, etc.
F
amount to a considerable sum. From our business point of view,
it is decidedly unnecessary for us to hold so large a quantity
G
of oil. It is quite obvious that the stockholding requirement

=== Page 327 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
Dr. Hornbeok.
Dec. 4/34.
"is for the sake of national defense. The Government therefore
ought to pay the expenses for this duty. All the oil companies
presented a joint appeal to the Government in this connection.
We are told that the Government is considering it."
While we were avare of the fact that the Japanese
companies were opposed to the stockholding regulations, this is the
first intimation we have had in New York that such opposition has
been so openly and strongly voiced by one who holds so prominent a
position as Mr. Hashimoto.
Sincerely yours,
Tumny F. Gor
KFC:M
Kersey F. Coe.

I encountered the British Ambassador at a lunoheon
today at the Chinese Legation. The Ambassador made some
casual remark about the petroleum situation in the Far
East. I took advantage of the opportunity to say that
I would like very much at some time to talk to him about

me about it and that he would take occasion when calling
at the Department within the next few days to drop in on
F/FG.
me. The Ambassador then remarked that Mr. Wiggin had

Ofrice had sent. I ventured the slight correction that
Mr. Wiggin had called me about the matter on the teiephone

=== Page 356 === (confidence 0.99)

the situation in 'Manchukuo'". Ne laughed in concert over
this, and I asked the Ambassador why, if that was what was

the Ambassador had ever heard the story of the little girl
who was scratching herself; someone asked her, "Why do you
scratch yourself?" She replied, "Because no one else
knows where I itch." The Ambassador said that this was
right to the point. I said it seemed to me that, on any
occasion when the British Foreign Office is prompted by
seeing news of such a character to inquire of us with
regard to the situation, they ought to feel free to tell us

At that point we were called into lunoheon.

CONFIDENTIA..
My British colleague has received a telegram from
London dated December 5 to the effect that the British
Foreign Office has discussed the general oil situation
with a representative of the State Department now in
London who has concurred in the following views.
(a) The petroleum monopoly in Manchuria und the
Jananese petroleum industry law are interrelated aspects
of the same problem and should be handled as such; and
(b) if a restriction of the export of oil is eventually
decided upon, the step should be taken by the oil
companies on a commercial basis leaving the respective
governments so far as possible in the background.
Clive is instructed to make renewed representations
to the Foreign Office concerning the effect on our com-
N
panies of the Japanese petroleum industry law but only
LED
after learning that I will take similar action. He has
prepared

=== Page 366 === (confidence 0.99)
MP
2-#271 From Tokyo, Dec. 8, 9 a.m.
prepared a provisional aide memoire which he has shown
to me in confidence and which he proposes to hand in-
formally (repeat informally) to the Vice Minister for
Foreign Affairs after hearing affirmatively from me.
Following is a summary of the British provisional
aide memoire. It is couched in courteous phraseology.
(one) British Governnent nas carefully studied
memorandum of Octcuer 31 exrlaining substance of law and
policy of its administration.
(two) Grateful for explanation and pleased that
Japanese authorities do not wish to deprive persons en-
gaged in this indusury of security for their business.
Explanation however has not removed anxiety lest wide
powers which the law confers might be so exercised as
in effect to constitute discrimination against interests
of British oil companies.
(three) While Japanese memorandum states that the
insistence upon annual submission of plans of operation
does not connote desire to hamper continuance (underline)
of business nevertheless such insistence would appear to
deprive British oil interests of all security as to scale
(end underline) of future operations. British interests
unable to count on sharing increase of trade while in the
event

=== Page 367 === (confidence 1.00)
MP
3-#271 From Tokyo, Dec. 8, 9 a me
event of a contraction of demand Japanese companies might
maintain their position while British quotas could be
reduced.
(four) Lack of British refineries in Japan and
uncertainty whether they would upon application be per-
mitted to refine upon a scale commensurate with their
present business seem to place British oil interests at a
disadvantage with Japanese companies. Japanese memorandum
states that esta lishment of refineries would be facili
tated for companies whose capital was predominantly
Japanese, but even this pernission may be withheld
in special circumstances, and in any case under such
financial arrangements British oil interests would for-
feit financial control of their company in Japan, a step
which they find themselves unable to contemplate.
(five) Sansom of British Embassy after consultation
with Dickover here inserts following observation which I
quote in full:
(SPECIAL GRAY) "(Foreign Office instructions are
to include an objection to those provisions of law which
provide for
(a) Purchase of petroleum from companies by
Government
(b) Interference by Government in selling price
of

=== Page 368 === (confidence 0.99)
MP
4-#271 From Tokyo, Dec. 8, 9 a.m.
of oil. I feel that we are on unsafe ground here. Any
government can properly comnandeer supplies in emergency,
at current prices, and there does not seem to me to be
any sinister implication in the use or the words military
affairs! as well as 'urgent necessity in public interest!.
Similarly as to price fix ng, it is not a discriminating
provision, as I read it.
I should say that cur only just ground of o jection
here is that, by their cumulative effect, such provisions
add to the uncertainty of the coma mies, - making it
difficult to decide future plans. I therefore suggest a
modification as follows: )
'While His Majesty's Government fully comprehend
the desire of Japanese Government to administer the
petroleum industry law for the protection of the public
welfare and for the maintenance of the interests of
industry as a whole, they feel that the provisions of
Article 7 of the law, together with Articles 7, 8 and
9 of the Imperial Ordinance for its enforcement give such
scope for official intervention which cannot be foreseen
as to make it difficult, if not impossible, for the
British oil interests to frame plans for their under-
takings with that degree of certainty as to future income
and

=== Page 369 === (confidence 0.99)
MP
5-#271 From Tokyo, Dec. 8, 9 a.m.
and expenditure which ordinary commercial practice entails.:"
(END SPECIAL GRAY)
(six) Japanese memorandum makes no reference to
the stock holding provisions of the law, which from the
standpoint of British oil interests are probably its most
onerous. As they stand they seem to empower the holding
of unlimited stocks. Prosent requirements are for 6
months stock but Minister of Commerce before Diet suggested
they might be extended to 12 months stock. British oil
interests have repeatedly informed British Government
that faced with doubt as to future sales, they are also
uncertain as to prioe for stock holding and possibility
of reimbursement for such expenditure, and consequently
are unable to decide whether from a commercial viewpoint
they are justified in further investment in Japan.
(seven) British Government feel that on basis of
information furnished by Japanese Government anxieties
of British oil interests are justifiable and that further
explanations given by officials of Departments of Commerce
and Foreign Affairs to representative of British oil
interests on November 20 were insufficient to remove those
anxieties. End of British aide memoire.
Please instruct.

One. In recent conversations between Kurusu of
the Japanese Foreign Office an: Moville of this Embassy
and Sansom of the British Enbassy, held at Kurusu's
request, he advanced proposal that, as the Japanese
petroleum law was oppressive for all oil conpanies,
Japanese as well as foreign, the foreign oil companies
should corbine with the Japanese cil companies in a
strong joint protest to the Ministry of Commerce and
Industry in an endeavor to obtain some modification of
the law. He intimated that the Japanese authorities
are beginning to realize the difficulties entailed by
the law and are weakening in their attitude and he men-
F/FG.
tioned the dissatisfaction of Japanese oil men with the
law.
1/6
Two. Department's 201, December 10, 8 p.m. In the
opinion of the British Empassy here the representations
FILE
which it proposes to make would not constitute an offi-
cial demarche but rather an informal paving of the way
for

=== Page 397 === (confidence 0.98)
FS
2-No. 274, December 13, from Tokyo
for the possible future discussions between the represen-
tatives of the foreign oil companies and the Japanese
authoritics, in that the proposed representations would
point out that the Japanese Government's reply of Octo-
ber 31 was insufficient to remove the anxieties felt by
the British oil interests, especially as the reply did
not refer to the question of stock holding.
Three. I feel that failure to inforri the Japanese
Government in some way that its reply tc our original
representations was unsatisfactory would be misleading
and might be misinterpreted. Asceptance without comment
of the Japanese reply would place the cil companies in a
less favorable positi on in their future negotiations with
the Japanese authorities because it could create the im-
pression that their governments were not continuing to
support them.
It appears as is indicated in paragraph one above,
the Japanese authorities are believed to be showing signs
of weakening. Further representations at the moment
therefore might be effective in preparing the way for
any compromise plan which the representatives of the for-
eign oil companies may evolve from their present discus-
sions at Shanghai.

=== Page 398 === (confidence 0.99)
FS
3-No. 274, December 13, from Tokyo
Five. It seems important both to Clive and myself
that our informal representations should be so formu-
lated as to avoid on the one hand creating an impression
that we are now going to let our companies work out their
own problem without further diplomatic support, and
equally to avoid on the other hand placing the Japanese
Government in a position which would make it more diffi-
cult for the authorities to recede and compromise with-
out serious loss of face.
Six. I therefore feel that it would be advisable
for us to go over the ground again in an informal con-
versation as envisaged in paragraph four of the Depart-
ment's telegram No. 0l, to be hald preferably between
the Counsellor of the Embassy and the Vice Minister for
Foreign Affairs or a bureau chief For the purposes of
clarity our points could be embodied,if the Department
approves, on an informal sheet of paper which could be
left with the Japanese official with the understanding
that our representations were oral and not formal.
Seven. The British Embassy is recommending to its
Foreign Office that the British representations be altered
to the same basis as those suggested above.
Eight. The following are the lines suggested for
the

=== Page 399 === (confidence 0.99)
FS
4-No. 274, December 13, from Tokyo
the informal and oral representations:
(a) After expressing appreciation of the explana-
tions made in the Japanese memorandum of October 31,
state that these explanations, as well as those which
it is understood were made orally by the Vice Minister
of Commerce and Industry on November 20, have not been
sufficient to dispel the anxieties felt by the American
o1l interosts.
(b) While noting the assurances given by the
Japanese Government that there is no intention, in the
granting of annual quotas, of ignoring the interests of
those engaged in the oil industry in Japan or of impair-
ing the continuous character of the industry, the American
Government still feels constrai ned to point out that the
wide powers conferred by the law in the granting of
quotas are such as to render it impossible for American
oil interests to gauge with any degree of certainty the
scale of their future business in Japan.
(c) It ap pears from the Japanese memorandum that
even if American oil interests are granted permission to
manufacture refined products in Japan, they wi ll not be
permittod to share in the natural growth of the industry
but will be restricted at best to the refining of an
amount

=== Page 400 === (confidence 0.99)
FS
5-No. 274, December 13, from Tokyo
.amount not exceeding their present import quotas, while
any increase in the demand will be allocated to the
Japanese refiners. The American Government expresses
the hope that the Japarese Government will see its way
clear to permit those interests to continue their busi-
ness on equal terms with the Japanese oil interests.
(d) The American Government notes that the Japanese
memorandum does not refer to the stock holding require-
ments of the petroleum industry law, which are considered
by the American cil interosts to be the most burdensome
provisions of the law. Because of the elastic nature of
these provisions, added to the uncertainty of future
sales and the lack of any assurance of compensation for
the increased investment entailed by the stock holding
provisions, the American oil interests are unable to
decide whether it is commercially feasible to invest
further funds in their business in Japan.
(e) The American Government therefore hopes that
the Japanese Government will again consider the problems
confronting the American oil interests in Japan and will
endeavor to remove the causes of their anxieties.
WSB:CSB

Dear Dr. Hornbeck:
While it is highly probable that Mr. Goold has
handed the Ambassador at Tokyo, for transmission to the State
Department, a transcript of notes of a recent interview be-
tween Mr. Yoshino and Mr. Kurusu and Mr. Coold and Mr. Ely,
concerning the Petroleum Law in Japan, we are nevertheless
taking the precaution of sending you these notes in duplicate
for we believe that if you have not seen them they will be of
considerable interest.
We feel it is becoming increasingly evident that
your efforts on our behalf, coupled with the endeavors made by
our own representatives in Japan, are resulting in a more temper-
ate viewpoint by at least certain of the officials concerned. If
this attitude is reflected in the deliberations of the Petroleum
Committee, and alsc of course, what is of more importance, in the
eventual decisions handed down by the Minister of Industry and
Commerce, we may possibly hope for modifications in the applica-
tion of the Law and Regulations which may permit of our operations
within the Japanese Empire, being placed upon a more satisfactory
basis than at present.
However, we are not unmindful of the fact that this
issue is still of grave importance to us, and consequently, we
are particularly pleased to learn from your letter of Dec. llth,
that further efforts on our behalf are being undertaken by the
State Department.
Sincerely yours,
Kumyf. Gor

Kersey F. Coe.

Ministry could proceed to the business of fixing quotas for l9s5.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
December 17, 1934.
No adtin requiid
Despatch No. 1073 of November 30, 1934,
from Tokyo.
The Embassy reports developments of the
oil situation in Japan.
The Japanese Government has declared
that no postponement of the statement of
business plans for 1935 will be permitted;
that penalties will follow failure to make
a report; that later changes in plans might
be approved; that the sales quota of the
Standard-Vacuum Oil Company could not be changed
mad this year; and that the diplomatic con-
versations now going on over the operation
of the Petroleum Law were an "entirely
separate matter".
The oil companies have decided, after
conferences with the diplomatic representa-
tives, to submit provisional plans, and have
been authorized to do so by their head
offices. The plans have been submitted,
accompanied by letters disclaiming responsi-
bility, and have been accepted by the
authorities.
The Japanese companies are experiencing
difficulties with the stock-storing pro-
visions of the Law, and are petitioning for
an

=== Page 431 === (confidence 0.96)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
an increase in gasoline prices. It is
believed that the attitude of the authorities
toward these provisions is weakening.
For an illuminating background picture
of the oil situation, the first enclosure
to the despatch coverinr an interview
between representatives of the Government
and the oil companies, is recommended.
