YOKOHAMA, 21/11/34
NOTES OF INTERVIEW BETWEEN MR. YOSHINO (VICE MINISTER OF COMMERCE
 INDUSTRY), MR. KURUSU (CHIEF OF THE BUREAU OF COMMERCIAL AFFAIRS,
FOREIGN OFFICE), AND MESSRS. GO0LD AND ELY, ON 20th NOVEMBER 1934.
Mr. Yoshino began by stating that there was no wish or
intention on the part of the Government that the foreign companies
should be driven out of Japan as the result of the enforcement of
the Petroleum Industry Law; on the contrary it was desired that
they should remain, and he wished therefore to make the following
points:-
(1) For the reasons which had been explained previously, the
Government considered it essential to sanction Plans of Undertaking
annually; but it would be possible for the foreign companies to
obtain a long term guarantee, say for ten years, of their present
trade in all products.
(During a private conversation after the interview Mr. Kurusu
expressed the opinion - which would have to be confirmed by the
Legal Bureau - that such a guarantee could be given by the Minister
of Commerce & Industry and made internationally binding by means of
official notifications through the Foreign Office to the Governments
of the companies concerned: it would have to be conditional in order
to obviate difficulties which might be caused by changes in the
relative offtakes of different products).
(2) The Government desired refining in Japan and therefore in
alloting trade quotas would give first preference to Refiners. By
"refiners" he meant Refiners as a whole: there would be no
discrimination whatsoever against refining enterprises controlled
by foreign capital.
(3) Applications for new refineries would have to go before the
Petroleum Committee for approval, but here again there would be no
disorimination against foreign capital, and applications would be
judged

=== Page 439 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
solely in the light of the domestic demand and supply
tuation. First preference would be given to importers of
refined produots who desired to convert their enterprises to
refining.
This statement of Mr. Yoshino's gave rise to a discussion of
the question of balancing products, as it seemed to Mr. Goold that
the Government so far had viewed refining solely from the standpoint
of the gasoline demand. Mr. Yoshino admitted that the situation
presented difficulties and stated that they would have to be met
by one of the following alternatives:-
(a) Export (of distress products), which would be desired
by Refiners but which, in his opinion, necessitated a duty refund;
this so far had been refused by the Finance Department, who had
already been approached by the Department of Commerce & Industry.
(b) An improvement in refining technique and practice which
would increase the proportion of gasoline extracted from raw material.
(c) Consideration of other products, and not gasoline only,
when giving permits for refining.
In short, the problem involved both technical and economic
considerations and was one that would have to go before the
Petroleum Committee, which now is expected to have its first
meeting early in December.
Here Mr. Kurusu interpolated - in regard to questions which
have to be considered by the Committee - that it seemed to him that
the foreign companies were "doing all the dirty work" in connection
with problems common to all the companies. Mr. Goold pointed out
that, notwithstanding the truth of Mr. Kurusu's remark, the foreign
companies were constantly being blamed and that his company had been
the object of attacks, which patently were inspired, in the vernacular
oil press, especially in regard to the current rate of their gasoline
sales. Both companies pointed out that to keep within the quotas
allotted

=== Page 440 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
to them by the Government for July/December they were
foroed to restrict sales (which were being made at full
nodule prices) and as a consequence were receiving strong protests
from their dealer organizations who had signified their intention
of complaining to the Ministry direct: and Mr. Goold pressed strongly
for an upward revision of the S.v.C's quota which, he stated, was
less than it should be even on the principle used by the Ministry
and represented a reduction in their trade.
After a long discussion Mr. Yoshino stated that the foreign
companies would have to accept and abide by the principle on which
the Ministry had based the July/December quotas; also that the quotas
allotted could not be changed: he promised however to give sympathetic
consideration to Mr. Goold's representations when fixing quotas for
1935. To overcome the difficulties which the foreign companies were
finding in keeping within their quotas and satisfying their dealer
organizations, he advocated closer cooperation generally with the
Japanese companies and a sales pool, in the formation of which the
Ministry was willing to assist.
In the course of this discussion Messrs. Goold and Ely drew
attention to the fact that the present level of gasoline prices was
definitely unremunerative. Mr. Yoshino informed them that the
Japanese companies had recently petitioned the Government's epproval
of an advance in price. The Ministry's first concern, when so
approached, was whether it was possible for the oil companies to
improve their position by economies rather than by raising prices -
and in this connection also he advocated the formation of a sales
pool as a means of reducing expenditure. He confirmed that the
Government did not contemplate allowing any other payment or
compensation for the cost of stock holding (which the Government
had estimated at about 1 Sen per A.G.) than could be recovered in
market prices and that the advance proposed by the Japanese companies
had included an allowance for that purpose.
Mr.

=== Page 441 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
Yoshino then requested the foreign companies to "cooperate
the existing system"; Messrs. Goold and Ely explained that the
whole situation was in the hands of their Governments and their
head offioes: they would be very glad to pass on what they had been
told and asked if Messrs. Yoshino and Kurusu had any further points
to elucidate.
Mr. Kurusu asked if the question of stock holding was holding
up & decision and, on being told that it was the principal difficulty,
indicated somewhat emphatically that the Japanese Government was
not going to be influenced by representations from the British and
U. S. Governments into altering its Petroleum Law or abolishing the
stock holding obligations. However, after some discussion with Mr.
Yoshino, he went on to say that in the opinion of the Japanese
Government no "concrete" representations concerning the stock
holding provisions of the Law had been received: but, if the
foreign companies investigated the cost and it should be specifically
represented with supporting figures - "from a commercial standpoint,
no politics" - that economically the burden was so great that, rather
than bear it, they would have to withdraw from Japan, then all
Departments of the Japanese Government would have to investigate
and reconsider the question in the light of alternative sources of
supply.
(Subsequently, in private conversation, Mr. Kurusu expressed
the personal opinion that if the foreign companies withdrew, or
even cut down their trade so that their present storage facilities
could meet the resultant reduced stock holding obligations, the
Japanese companies would not be able to bridge the gap and Japan
would be faced with a shortage of supply. He inferred that so
strong an attitude on the part of the foreign companies would assist
him and Mr. Yoshino to influence other Government Departments which
were insisting on upholding the stock keeping provisions of the law;
and added incidentally that the British and U. S. Embassies would
mow how best to phrase representations).
m

=== Page 442 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
conclusion Mr. Yoshino brought up the matter of the foreign
mpanies' inoomplete Plans of Undertaking for l935. Messrs. Goold
and Ely explained their position and stated that their applications
for postponement had been definitely refused by the Mining Bureau,
which had threatened the imposition of penalties. After some dis-
cussion Mr. Yoshino stated that if the Returns were made conditionally -
the fulfillment of import programmes to cover sales and stock keeping
being made subject to the companies' decision as to whether or not
it was economically possible for them to carry on business in Japan
under the Petroleum Law - they would be accepted. It would have
to be understood however that in these circumstances any quotas
allotted by the Government to the foreign companies would also be
conditional. He pressed the companies to complete their Returns
in that manner and on that understanding, so that the Ministry
could proceed to the business of fixing quotas for 1935.

FILED
a conversation held on November 30, 1934, between Mr. Ne-

Foreign Office. In this conversation Mr. Kurusu indicated
that

=== Page 478 === (confidence 0.98)
-8 -
that the Japanese oil companies were dissatisfied with
the Petroleum Industry Law and desired some modification,
adding that the oil companies should act as a unit in this
regard. In a late: conversation with Mr. Sansom, of the
ritish Embassy, Mr. Kurusu expressed his idea more clearly.
According to Mr. Sansom, Mr. Kurusu stated that, since the
law was in fact an oppressive law for all the companies,
the best solution would be for all the oil companies,
Japanese as wcll as for ign, to combine in a strong pro-
test to the Ministry of Commerce and Industry. The JIJI
SHIMP0 of Decomber 13, 1934, st tes that the Japanese oil
companies wil? need some Yen 60,000,000 for the construc-

they are unable to raise this sum. They are thcrefore pe-
titioning the Minist y of Comerce and Industry for permis-

some sort of subsidy.
While the matter of a combined protest from the Japan-
ese and foreign oil companies has not yet becn thoroughly
discussed by the oil companies and their respective Embassies,
this Embassy can perceive no objection to the plan, which
it appears would do no harm and night be effective in ob-
taining some modification of themore oppressive provisions
of the law.

=== Page 479 === (confidence 0.99)

2/
There is also enclosed a summary, made in the Embassy,
of a series of articles which were published in the TOKYo
ASAHI on November 6,8,11,14 and 18, 1934, on the subject
of the protests lodged by the United States and Great Bri-
tain against Japan's Petroleum Industry Law. The writer
deals with the subjects of the possible suspension of sup-
plies of oil to Japan by the large foreign oil companies
and the suoply of substitutes for liquid fuel in Japan and
Manchuria. His general opinion is that Japan need not fear
the suspension of supplies of oil to Japan by British, Ameri-
can and Netherlands companies, as supplies are available
from other sources and fuel substitutes can be developed
in Japan and Manchuria. He concludes with an interesting
statement to the effect that an oil monopoly system may be
established in Japan in the future, and that the oil monopoly
in Manchuria is intended to serve as a test case.

Conversation:
December 4, 1934.
Mr. Dickover and Mr. Kojiro Matsukata,
of the Matsukata Nisso Oil Company.
I lunched at the Tokyo Club to ay and after lunch
had a talk with Kojiro Matsukata, of the Matsukata Nisso
Oil Company (handling Soviet gasoline). Mr. Matsukata
wanted to know why the for ign oil conpanies did not drop
their antagonistic attitude toward the Japanese Govern-
ment and try to reach some compromise. I said that the
protests which had been made were not based on legalis-
tic grounds and that some compromise, which would put the
oil trade in Japan on a reasonably profit ble commercial
basis, might be quite acceptable. He suggested that the
foreign oil companies join with the Japanese oil companies
in petitioning for a higher price for gasoline. I pointed
out that such a solution would not obviate the necessity
for the investment by the foreign firms of very large sums
in Japan-a step which the oil companies were not willing
to take under the uncertain conditions of trade imposed
by the Petroleum Law. He asked what other solution was
possible - would the foreign oil companies withdraw from
Japan and lose their already large investments? I said
that the conditions imposed by the law might be made less
onerous - for instance, the stock-holding requirements
might be changed from six months to three and a guarantee
of future business be given. He said that he thought
that that would be impossible, and again asked why a
raise

=== Page 481 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
raise in the gasoline price would not be sufficient.
I said that in addition to the large investment in
tanks and oil which still would be required, there was
no certainty that the yen would remain at its present
level and that the increase in ric? would therefore
be an uncertain return for a certain expenditure;
moreover, that the taxi and bus companies would probably
make it so unpleasant for the Government that the incr ase
in price might be rescinded at any time and thereby des-
troy the compensation which the companies expected to
obtain for investments already made. He then suggested
that, as a compromise plan, the foreign oil companies
proceed to store three months oil by April 1, 1935, and
after that petition the Government to be released from
further storage. I said tht I doubted that the oil com-
panies would consent to such a compromise, as they would
thereby be in the position of having accopted the stock-
holding requirements, without any surety that they would
not be required to carry the full six-months' stock.
Mr. Matsukata later asked why the oil men did not
come to Japan and talk things over with the Japanese Govern-
ment instead of going to Shanghai for a conference. I
said that the local representatives of the oil companies
had talked things over with the Government, but it must
be remembered that they were subordinates who had no au-
thority to decide big questions. He said that the meeting
of the big oil men in Shanghai and not in Japan indicated
antagonism to the Japanese Government. I said that if it
was

=== Page 482 === (confidence 0.98)
-3 -
was true that they were to confer in Shanghai, I did
not believe that it indicated any antagonism but merely
a coincidence - that it happened that some were coming
from the west and some from the east and they would meet
quicker in Shanghai than in Japan.
Mr. Matsukata gave me the impression that he was
feeling around for some sort of a solution of the problem -
on whose behalf was not indicated. Also, that he was
very anxious to draw the foreign oil companies into the
petition for an increase in the price of gasoline, as
such increase would be of great benefit to his own business.
E.R. Dickover

Summary of an unsigned series of articles
published in the TOKYO ASAHI of November 6,
8,11,14,and 18, 1934, entitled "Protest by
the Unitea Ste te: nd Great Brit in ugainst
Jupan's Petrcleum Incustry Lav".
The lodging of protests by the United Stetes, Great
Britain and the Netherland: against the oil control laws
of Japan and  anchuria simultaneously une :s it  re-arranged
amazed the Japanese peo le. Being lougeu t the time or the
preliminary naval conversations, their motive is obvious.
As regerds the sales quotas given the oil firms, for-
terly under the voluntary cartel system the foreign firms
hanclea co per cent of the gasoline sela in J in and the
Japanese refineries 40 per cent; now the proportion is about
50 per cent and 50 per cent. The object of the law is not
to jeopardize vested interests of the foreign oil companies,
but to increase the proportion of the traue hanuled by the
Japanese firms, by utilizing the natural increase in demand
for this purpose. Another object is to employ the iale refining
equipment in Japan, and to inauce the foreign firms to refine
in Japan instead of i porting refined prodncts. The foreign
oil firms hold, however, that to deny then the same propor-
tion of the total trade as they had handled in the past is
tantamount to a reduction in quotas.
The Petroleum Industry Law also imposes a system of
storing oil in times of peace, for purposes of national
defense.

=== Page 484 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
defense. The foreign oil companies are opposed to this
system.
The protests in regard to the Manchuria oil monopoly
state that the Oil Monopoly Law and the Manchuria Oil
Company Law are opposed to the principles of the open door
and equal opport nity as contained in the Nine-Power Treaty.
This is purely a commercial problem which should not have
been taken up aiplomatically. The Manchuria Oil Company
is not intended to monopolize the oil trade of Manchuria,
nor will the Manchukuo" Government monopolize oil imports.
The protests made by the United States and British
Governments were made solely on behalf of the Standard Oil
and Royal Dutch-Shell interests. (Here follow statistics
indicating that of the gasoline imported into Jupan in 1933
the Rising Sun Petroleum Company handled 50 per cent, the
Standard-Vacuum Oil Company 40 per cent, and the Matsukata
Nisso Oil Company 4 per cent, while 6 per cent was manu-
factured in bond. The Japanese oil refineries during the
same period produced gasoline to the amount of 88 per cent
of the imported gasoline.) The Standard Oil Company started
operations in Japan in 1890, the Rising Sun Petroleum
Company in 1900, the Nippon Oil Company (by amalgamation of
several small Japanese refineries) in 192l, and the Ogura
Oil Company in 1925. (Here follow more or less accurate
histories of the rise of the Standard and Shell companies.)
The question arises as to the ability of the foreign
oil companies to compel attention by the Japanese Government
to the protests made by the British and American Governments,
through the suspension of supplies of oil to Japan.
Concerted

=== Page 485 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3 -
Concerted action by the oil companies seems to be out of
the question.
In addition to the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company and
the Rising Sun Petroleum Company, Japan has as suppliers
of crude oil the Rio Grande Oil Company, the Associated
Oil Company, Richfield Oil Company, Union Oil Company,
Texas Oil Company and the Standard Oil Company of Cali-
fornia. It is not believed tha all of these will consent
to take concerted action in association with the Standard-
Vacuum Oil Company and the Rising Sun Petroleum Company.
The supply of oil throughout the world exceeas the demand,
so there is no need to fear a boycott. Rumania, Persia and
Central and South American countries wish to sell oil to
uig!?
Japan. The Rumanian price is high, but not so his as entirely
to preclude the use of Rumanian crude oil in Japan. Japan
needs about 2,000,000 tons of crude oil annually to supply
its demand for refined products. Of this, S37,000 tons are
produced in Japan and about 200,000 tons are obtained from
Japanese wells in North Saghalien. Japanese companies are
also drilling for oil in the Netherlands Indies.
The World War taught the value of oil to all the coun-
tries of the world, and consequently nearly all countries
have now adopted oil control las. The tendency is to control
oil more rigidly than ever before. The oil monopoly law of
"Manchukuo" and the Petroleum Industry Law of Japan were
drafted with the French and Spanish oil control la s as models.
(Here follows a brief description of the French and Spanish
1aws.)
Japan

=== Page 486 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
Japan must encourage the production of substitutes
for fuel oil, such as low temperature distillation of coal.
To supply Japan's needs for fuel oil in this way, some
10,000,000 tons of coal will be needed annually. Alcohol
is available in Japan but is expensive and requires a special
type of engine for its use alone. The Japanese,Navy, however,
has developed a fuel for airplanes and motor-cars consisting
of 50 per cent gasoline, 15 per cent alcohol and 35 per cent
benzol. The chief source of alcohol in Japan would be Taiwan
molasses, from which about 8,000,000 gallons of alcohol per
annum could be obtained. In "Manchukuo" experiments are being
made with algohol fuel obtained from Kaoliang and Indian corn.
Crude oil is also obtainable from Fushun shale. At present
output is about 75,000 tons, most of which is sold to the
Navy at a price about Yen 9.00 above the current prices for
natural crude oil. Manchuria thus could be self-sunplying
as regards fuel oils, but the industry needs encouragement.
In the future the Japanese Government ay take advantage
of a suitable opportunity to establish n oil monopoly system
in Japan, in order to make oil contril more effective than
it is now. Some think that the oil monopoly law in force in
"Manchukuo" at present is intended to serve as a test.
